The question that forced it: eleven grants with expiries scattered across 08-18..08-25 meant something died every week on a system meant to run unattended for months, and the two nearest lapses (google_ai_studio, telegram on 08-18) would have taken the whole hosted chain and the notification channel with them. Three capabilities had already left grants one at a time (live trading 2026-07-28, candle archive 2026-08-04, Naver lane 2026-08-09), each pricing the same ledger: what a grant buys (a second factor, an expiry, an audited per-machine scope record, file-deletion revocation, self-decay of a copied state dir) against what its renewal costs (a gap that blocks a CLOSE path or silently holes a rolling collection). Thomas priced the rest the same way and chose to retire renewal outright — the alternatives (longer TTLs, aligned expiry dates, an expiry-warning preflight) were presented and declined. **What the conversion preserves, deliberately:** construction order (the capable implementation is still built only behind the opt-in, receiving its `Authorization`), egress re-checks (they re-read the env var), the inert default on unset/unknown values, and the chain rules — an unknown or duplicate member still fails the whole selection closed, and `select_env_gated_chain` pins the FULL chain string into every member's `env_gate` so *editing* the composition revokes every member at its next egress, which is what replaced deleting one member's grant file. The M2 tiers gained `MVP_OPENROUTER_TIERS` (an explicit comma list) because they were the one place grant-retirement would have failed OPEN: their fallback is degrade-to-base, so with no opt-in of their own, removing the grant check would have silently armed all three tiers — unset on the live machine, so behavior is unchanged (degrade). The containment test inverted without changing its job: it now asserts zero callers of the retired grant selectors (restoring grants must be a named decision) AND the exact env-gated call-site list (a new capability still cannot ship ungoverned); `_GATE_ENV_VARS` grew to the full opt-in roster since each var now selects a REAL implementation in any suite that inherits it. The grant machinery (`authorize`, `build_activation_record`, `select_gated*`, `activate_safety_flag.py`, the record-minting unit tests) is retained with zero runtime callers pending its own removal PR. Read `safety_gate.py`'s module docstring before restoring any of it — it lists what was given up, on purpose, so the restoration argues against the real ledger and not a strawman. (`crypto/live_leg.py`, `crypto/live_route.py`, `tests/test_mvp_runtime_crypto_external_close_settlement.py`; #631, incident 2026-08-08, shipped 2026-08-09). Thomas flattened an open BTCUSDT position from the Binance UI at market. The runtime saw it correctly — `venue_closed: true`, both bracket legs `EXPIRED` with no fill — and then could not settle it, because `settle_venue_closed_position` priced the exit from whichever bracket leg filled and no leg filled: **the bracket is not what closed the position.** The consequences were not local. The book stayed `OPEN`, so reconciliation refused new entries on the symbol; the outcome was never written, so the trade was invisible to the loss breakers and to every R-denominated limit; and because `EXIT_UNSETTLEABLE` is in `_INCIDENT_STATUSES`, live routing halted across **every** context, not just BTCUSDT 4h. Measured on this machine: 93 cycles over 23 hours, 2026-08-08T07:43:55Z to 2026-08-09T06:58:51Z, with no operator tool to resolve it and no automatic path out — the only exits available were leaving live halted indefinitely or editing the book against a fabricated price, which is exactly what the function's docstring refuses. **The operational trap is worth more than the bug.** Throughout those 23 hours `breaker_watch_mark.json` read `status: NORMAL` / `allow_new_position: true`. The breakers and the route watch are different doors, and a healthy breaker board says nothing about whether live is halted; when live is stopped, read `route_watch_mark.json` first, because the board that looks like the answer is not. **The authority was already there and nothing had read it:** `account.AccountFeed.fill_history` (`/fapi/v1/userTrades`) returns the account's own fills with `qty`, `quoteQty` and a side per row — the venue's record of the very close that happened — and it was consumed only by fee measurement. So when no bracket leg can price the exit, the exit is rebuilt from the fill history and run through the *same* `realized_pnl_usdt` every other exit uses, which is what makes an externally closed trade and a bracket-closed one the same kind of record. **This is not "invent instead of refuse" — the refusals are the design.** Every number comes from the venue; what was added is a rule for deciding *which* fills closed this position, and it declines rather than guesses: closing-side only, at or after the open, oldest-first until they exactly cover the quantity; a fill that overshoots the remainder refuses the whole answer (the symbol saw activity that cannot be attributed) and a total that never reaches the quantity refuses too (a partial close is still an open position). Refusing keeps the book `OPEN`, which is both the prior behaviour and the correct consequence of not knowing, and `fill_history` returning `None` (no feed) stays a different reason code from `[]` (no fills). `venue_external_close` is a **new** close reason, deliberately not folded into `stop_loss` or `time_exit`: those name a strategy rule ending, and an operator's decision does not belong in the population the R statistics judge a strategy on. **It settled itself.** Merged 06:49:01Z, deployed 06:54:49Z, and on the next cycle at 06:58:51Z the position closed at 64923.8 off the venue's own fill for +0.6538 USDT / +0.939R with both legs cancelled `already_gone`, no operator CLI in the loop; the route went back to `HELD`, which is the lifecycle ladder, not to firing. Two things the entry exists to carry. **The premise was an inference for a day** — "the operator flattened it" was deduced from `venue_closed: true` with neither leg filled, and was only confirmed first-hand by Thomas on 2026-08-09, *after* the squash merge; the confirmation reached the PR body 13 seconds too late to be in the commit message, which is why it is written down here instead. And **a manual close is not a thing the system can be told**: the operator saying it happened puts nothing on the ledger, because the ledger only ever learns an exit price from the venue's own fills. Nor is this a one-off — ETHUSDT reached the same state on 2026-08-05 and cleared only because a bracket leg later happened to fill, which is luck, not a mechanism. What #631 did *not* finish was caught and closed the same day: `exit_source` reached the cycle record but not the outcome row in `live_outcomes.jsonl` that the breakers actually read, so a consumer holding only that row was still inferring provenance from `close_reason` — and that inference was wrong in both directions, since a `venue_external_close` could in principle be priced by a bracket leg and a `stop_loss` priced from the fill history is exactly what this runtime now produces. #635 threads the value onto the row itself, with three names for three paths (`runtime_close`, `bracket_leg`, `fill_history`).

- **The live hard ceiling moved for the first time, and the interesting part is everything it did not move** (`crypto/live_budget.py`, `schemas/live_trading_budget.v0.1.schema.json`, `scripts/register_live_trading_budget.py`, `tests/test_mvp_runtime_crypto_live_budget.py`; 2026-08-08). `HARD_CEILING_USDT` 200 → 500 on Thomas's instruction, after the live path had earned 4 clean canary orders and completed 2 real round trips. **What this is:** the largest number a *registered budget* may declare. **What it is not:** a cap any order is judged against. The guard reads `min(max_order_notional_usdt, absolute_max_notional_usdt)` off the registered record, so until an operator re-registers, every order is still judged against the caps already in the file — this change authorizes nothing on its own, which is the only reason it can land as a normal PR rather than an operator step. **Two authorities pin the number** — the builder (so the refusal names the offending value instead of surfacing a raw schema error) and the schema (so a hand-edited record on disk is invalid too) — and nothing made them agree. Raise one and not the other and the *lower* silently becomes the real ceiling, with which one that is depending on whether a record arrived through the builder or through the file; `test_schema_and_code_agree_on_the_hard_ceiling` is now the thing that notices. The refusal test was rewritten against the constant (`HARD_CEILING_USDT + 1`) so it stops encoding the number, and `test_hard_ceiling_is_the_operator_approved_number` pins the literal instead: moving a money boundary should read as a deliberate edit to a test whose name says so, not a one-character constant bump. **The script's defaults were deliberately left at the bring-up values**, `--absolute-max-notional 200` included. A default is what an *omitted* flag registers, and an omitted flag must never be the thing that widens a limit; every cap past the starting values is now typed out by the operator. The same paragraph records the footgun found while checking this: re-registration replaces the whole record rather than the flags passed, so an omitted `--symbols` narrows a five-symbol allowlist to BTCUSDT and an omitted `--min-clean-canary-orders` drops the promotion bar from 4 to 3 — both silently, both on a machine where autonomous routing is wired. **The evidence the raise rests on, stated plainly because it is thin:** at the time of the change the live ledger held 2 closed trades, both stopped out, −1.86 USDT total. The path is proven; the edge is not, and a ceiling is not a forecast.

- **The three assistant doors assumed four things about how a door stands, and each assumption had a failure behind it** (`socket_door.py`, `bridge_idempotency.py`, `switch_bridge.py`, `dispatch_bridge.py`, `read_bridge.py`, `store.py`, `retention.py`, `scripts/check_test_skips.py`; 2026-08-05). From an architecture review of the Hermes wiring; the review's own top finding was a misreading (it took the policy's `runtime_effect` block — which classifies *that record's* effect and is documented as doing so — for a claim about system state), but four of its transport findings reproduce in the code and are fixed here. **(1) Binding removed whatever socket file it found**, on the reasoning that "a live server holds no lock on the path". That is true and is exactly why it was unsafe: unlinking a pathname does not close the server bound to it, so a double start left the old process serving its established connections while every new connection reached the new one — two doors, one contract, and no way to tell which answered, during the restart being performed because something was already wrong. The path is now probed (`door_is_live`) and a live one is refused with `BRIDGE_ALREADY_RUNNING`; uncertainty answers "live", because a refused start names a path an operator can clear and a wrong "dead" makes two doors. **(2) A thread per connection with no ceiling**, on a socket whose client is a model that retries. Now a fixed pool per door — read 8, switch 4, dispatch 2 — with a small admission slack in front of it, and past *that* a connection is answered `BRIDGE_BUSY` and closed rather than left to queue indefinitely. The slack is not decoration: a handler releases its slot after the reply is sent, so a client that reads its answer and immediately reconnects can arrive while the slot is still held, and at a ceiling of 1 a strictly **sequential** caller — the only kind these doors have — collided with itself often enough to fail a test on a loaded machine. Admission and execution are therefore bounded separately. The accept loop never waits for a slot, deliberately: a flood that made the loop block would stall the legitimate connections behind it, which is the outage the ceiling exists to prevent rather than cause. The switch door is deliberately **not** 1: it carries the stop *and* the start, the start is the slow one, and a ceiling of one would let an in-flight `enable` refuse the `disable` behind it — the exact failure that made this door its own service, reproduced inside it. **(3) `str(exc)` went back to the caller** for any exception a door did not anticipate — store paths, config, dependency text, to the least trusted end of the socket. The reply is now a stable `BRIDGE_ERROR` with an `error_ref`, and the traceback goes to the service log under the same ref; the reason_code is unchanged on purpose, because `docker-compose.yml` names it as the signature of the #380 mount failure. **(4) File permissions were the whole of authentication**, and they authorize a *group*: every process in it is identical to `chmod` and different to `SO_PEERCRED`, which the kernel records at connect() and no frame can choose. `MVP_BRIDGE_CLIENT_UID` adds the uid check, unset by default (a list nobody configured would refuse the operator's own shell first) and fail-closed when configured but unanswerable. One ordering decision is worth knowing before "tightening" it: the peer check runs **after** the frame is drained and before it is decoded. Checking first was tried and is worse — replying while the peer is still writing closes a socket with data pending, which reaches the client as a reset, so a door that checks first cannot actually *tell* an unpermitted peer that it was unpermitted. **Also here, and separately: `request_id`.** A client that times out cannot distinguish "failed" from "still running", so the honest thing for it to do is retry — and without an identity on the frame the door ran the effect twice. `bridge_idempotency` claims an id before the effect and completes it after, so a repeat replays the recorded outcome instead of minting a second approval request or billing a second analysis to the shared quota. It is optional (mandatory would refuse every existing caller for a property most do not need), the read door is **not** wired to it (reads have no effect to duplicate), and `disable` is not either — an emergency control you must first acquire a file lock for is a worse trade than the duplicate it would prevent. The crash window between claim and completion is stated in the module rather than papered over: it is bounded by a TTL and visible in the file, and closing it entirely needs a transaction across two stores this deployment does not have. The rows live on `LedgerStore` as a ninth file rather than in a new store, and the rotation decision is explicit — rotatable, because every row carries an `expires_at` shorter than the rotation horizon. **What the review asked for and did not get, with the reason:** a single `ExecutionStage` enum replacing the live-order gates. Those gates — env opt-in, confirmation phrase, registered budget, risk limits, both kill switches, canary evidence — are independent and all must hold; deriving them from one enum turns "eight things must be true" into "one thing must be true", which is a smaller safety property wearing a tidier shape.

- Also done: **A green pytest that stopped running 208 tests was still green** (`scripts/check_test_skips.py`, `tests/skip_ceiling.json`; 2026-08-05). **The two workflow edits that call it are NOT in this change** — the token that pushed it has no `workflow` permission, so `.github/workflows/{mvp-runtime-tests,thomas-agent-runtime-validation}.yml` need `--junitxml=junit.xml` on their pytest step and a following `python scripts/check_test_skips.py --junit junit.xml` step (with `if: always()`) applied by someone whose credentials carry it. Until then the script exists and nothing calls it. `tests/_helpers.requires_local_core` gates 208 tests on a local Core activation, and both workflows run `ci_activate_core_for_tests.py` first precisely so they run rather than skip. Measured at 3c31bf8: with the activation **4383 passed / 2 skipped**, without it **4175 passed / 210 skipped** — and the second one is green, exit 0, indistinguishable in the final line unless you read the count, which nothing did. The check reads a junit report and fails on a skip whose *reason* cannot be legitimate in CI, which needs no baseline and cannot drift. A total per-platform ceiling is the second, weaker half and ships **unset**: the two skips a developer sees locally are `root can chown to any gid`, which a non-root CI runner does not skip at all, so a number measured on this host would be wrong on both platforms. The file says to record it from a CI run and the check reports the observed count until someone does — a ceiling asserted from a number nobody measured is the kind of check that gets disabled the first time it is wrong.

- **The gross edge this store was mining IS the overfit, and the factory rotation moved off 15m onto 1d** (measurement only — no code change; `.runtime_governance_state/schedules.jsonl`; 2026-08-04). The 2026-08-02 entry below records *"gross edge is nearly flat across the ladder (+0.0923R at 15m, +0.1368R at 1h, +0.1517R at 4h) while cost varies 4.4×"*. Those gross figures are **in-sample**. Measured out of sample over **426 holdout blocks at the current cost basis, 40,665 trades** — `backtest_evidence.holdout` carries every cost leg in R, so gross is recoverable as net + costs/trade — the ladder reads **+0.0259R at 15m, +0.0027R at 1h, +0.0046R at 4h, aggregate +0.0124R against 0.1567R of cost for −0.1765R net.** The cost figures reproduce closely; the gross figures are 10–30× smaller. **That gap is not a discrepancy to reconcile, it is the finding:** essentially the entire apparent gross edge is selection on noise, and cost is not eating a real edge — it is turning a zero negative. Three consequences that are each easy to get backwards. **4h is not the better tier because its entries are better** — its gross (+0.0046R) is indistinguishable from 1h's (+0.0027R); it nets best because it pays a third of the friction per R, which is a property of the denominator and not of the signal. **No per-family retirement is coherent on this evidence.** Collapsed to one row per lineage (274 single-family lineages at the current basis), nearly every family sits within ±0.03R of zero gross; a rule strong enough to retire the worst — t < −3 on the lineage mean — takes ten families including `breakdown_short` (43 lineages, 4,867 holdout trades, t = −6.54), which is the parent of two of the five strategies currently PAPER_ACTIVE. `volatility_squeeze_*` was retired on in-sample net, a weaker instrument than this and pointing at a mechanism; there is no comparable mechanism story for the other ten, only a library-wide zero. **And widening `stop_atr` halves cost without moving gross** (0.2133R → 0.1154R across the bands; gross stays +0.01 ± 0.01), so the floor that moved 0.8 → 1.2 on 2026-08-02 makes the loss smaller and cannot make a profit — which is what that entry already said about the fast end, now measured at every tier. **Why the promotable board reads 0 against 1,140 candidates, as a partition:** 119 already active, 546 refused on cost basis, 409 holdout INSUFFICIENT, 66 holdout CONTRADICTED — and the quality filters (`verdict`, `expectancy`) refused **zero**, because nothing reaches them. Of the 480 at the current basis, **360 carry no `stdev_r` on their holdout block** (minted before GEN-740) and **216 of those already have ≥25 holdout trades** — refused for a recording reason rather than a performance one, and self-draining as the factory re-mints. Of the 115 that can be judged, 66 clear the trade floor, and their **best t is +0.547 with a median of −1.067**; the nearest candidate to confirmation would need 462 holdout trades against the 36 it has. The selection correction is wired and running, not pending: attempts are recorded (70 per search context, a corrected bar of 3.38σ), and of the 118 rows that can be ranked, **9 clear the uncorrected 1.96 and 0 clear 3.38.** **The one change made on the strength of this is operational.** The factory rotation is now 5 symbols × {1h, 4h, 1d}: the five `<SYM> 15m` schedules are disabled and five `<SYM> 1d` ones added. 15m pays 0.2480R of friction against +0.0259R of out-of-sample gross — a 10× gap no hit rate closes — and the 2026-08-02 entry had explicitly left this decision open pending exactly this number. 1d was not stopped for performance: it fell out on 2026-07-23 when the per-context rotation replaced the generic schedule and simply omitted it, and `MIN_FACTORY_BARS = 2000` exists specifically to give it a scoreable sample. Dry-run verified before the first fire (the scheduler's own fetch + `run_factory` with `append_candidates` withheld): 2,000 daily bars come back (2021-02-11 → 2026-08-03), 30 templates mint, 8 candidates build, and `positioning_eligible` is False as expected. **1d does not fix judgeability** — its holdout trade counts came out 5/7/18/19/21/25/30/32, straddling `MIN_HOLDOUT_TRADES = 25` exactly as 4h does. It is minted for the cost denominator, and that is the whole claim. **Two things this deliberately does not license.** It is not a case for adding another template family: the last three genuinely new information sources — taker flow, session, cross-section — all landed at gross ≈ 0 (+0.02 to −0.03R), so "add a source" now has a measured record of three for three. And it is not a decision on limit entry; taker fee is 62% of all cost and slippage 34%, which is the evidence the deferred limit-entry decision was waiting for — but re-opening it is Thomas's call and its four standing conditions are unchanged by this. **Recorded here because the rotation change leaves no other trace:** `schedules.jsonl` is per-machine state and never committed, so `git log` will not show it and a fresh clone will not have it. Re-enabling 15m is `scheduler_cli enable <id>` on the five disabled rows, which are still on file.

- Also done: **The cache-validity check on every record validation cost more than it needed to, and the rest of the review is recorded rather than done** (`schema_cache.py`, `docs/REMAINING_WORK.md`; 2026-08-03). `_directory_signature` runs on **every** validation — it is what decides whether the cached generation may be reused — and a run validates 15-20 records against 75 schemas. It used `Path.glob` + `Path.stat`, building a `Path` object and an `fnmatch` call per entry on top of the syscalls. `os.scandir` produces a **byte-identical** signature from one directory read: measured **0.411 ms → 0.180 ms per call** on the live host, ~7.4 ms → ~3.2 ms per run. Same lesson as the note it replaces, one layer out — that one had found `path.stat()` caches nothing, so reading size and mtime separately was doubling the check. **What was rejected matters more than what was done:** the remaining ~75 stat syscalls are the floor, not an oversight. This module promises that ANY change to the schemas directory — edit, add, remove — misses the cache, which is what makes a stale validator impossible and is pinned by `test_any_directory_change_invalidates_the_cache`. The directory's own mtime is one syscall and would have bought the last 3.2 ms, but it does not move when a file is edited **in place**; detecting that means asking every file, and asking every file is 75 syscalls. Written into the docstring so the next person to find this hot path does not re-derive the trade and quietly take the cheaper half. **The review that produced this is now `REMAINING_WORK.md` section G**, recorded there rather than in a chat log because that file is the one that travels between machines: three items open (**G1** 602 crypto tunables across 42 modules with no owner — the class that already produced the "0 promotable against 900 candidates" incident, and explicitly *not* to be done as one sweep of the money path; **G2** the 3,311-LOC dead capability lane, safe and mechanical and not urgent; **G3** 1,188 reason codes with no index), three closed and named so they are not re-investigated, and three considered and deliberately declined with the reasoning kept. The audit chain's unrotated tip scan is recorded under *do not fix this* — 904 events, 2.7 MB, ~20 ms, growing ~20 events a day. The per-machine hand-off section is now concrete for the same reason: which twelve grants this deployment holds, that the twenty-two registered schedules do not travel, and that the canary count and paper P&L gating the live door read empty on a fresh machine however far along this one is.

- **REMOVED: the whole prediction-market lane (PM-series)** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/predmarket/` and everything wired to it; 2026-08-02). Thomas: prediction-market trading is not a lane this project may operate under **Korean domestic regulation**. **Deleted rather than disabled, and that is the substance of the decision** — a disabled capability is one grant away from running, and this one must not be reachable by an operator who later finds a stale `MVP_KALSHI_MARKET_DATA` in `.env` and wonders what it does. Removed: the 6,746-LOC package (three venue adapters, screening, the deterministic matcher, the fee-adjusted detector, the observation store, the proposal record, the exit report), nine test files (5,478 LOC), `scripts/backfill_predmarket_topic_ids.py`, the roadmap, the `pm_scan` scheduler kind and both its cadences, the `/pred` console verb and its authority line in `operator.py`, the unused `LiveLikePredMarketCollector` test helper, and the five venue selectors in `docker-compose.yml`. The compose lines went too rather than being left "harmlessly": Compose forwards **only** what its `environment:` block names, so a name there is the whole difference between a key in `.env` reaching a container and reaching nothing, and a test now pins their absence from *both* services — the inverse of the test that used to pin their presence, for the same reason it existed (nothing errors either way, so only a test says which state the deployment is in). **The per-machine observation store was deleted too** — 669 MB, 205,079 readings, gitignored and therefore unrecoverable; that was asked and answered explicitly rather than assumed. **What PM1 had measured, kept because it is what 5.7 days of a 14-day window bought:** 205,079 readings across 76 confirmed groups at ~0.995 pricing coverage, and the detector's own verdict was still `INSUFFICIENT_WINDOW` at closure — so **PM1 never reached a go/no-go on whether the edge was real.** The window was cut short by a regulatory finding, not by a negative result, and anyone revisiting this must treat the question as unanswered rather than answered "no". **The removal exposed a defect, which is the part worth carrying forward.** `scheduler._execute` tested each kind in turn and then *fell through* to the analysis pipeline — so a schedule of any unrecognised kind ran a real model call on its `request` text, reported as a normal `COMPLETED` fire. Nothing could reach that while the code that wrote the schedule store and the code that read it were the same version; **removing a kind is exactly when they stop being.** The two live `pm_scan` rows were removed from the store in the same change, but a machine restored from an older state directory still has them, and the 2-minute one would have billed an LLM analysis of the string `"watch"` every two minutes. An unknown kind is now refused by name (`UNKNOWN_KIND`), and two tests pin the split that makes the removal survivable: a stale row still **loads** (`_from_record` reads `kind` as a string and does not check `KINDS`, so one dead schedule cannot take `crypto_pipeline` down with it) and is **refused at dispatch**. Fail-closed on run, survivable on read.
- Also done: **The backtest scored trades the runtime refuses, and it was pessimistic exactly where it mattered** (`crypto/factory.py`; 2026-08-02). `cost.MAX_ENTRY_COST_R` refuses a plan whose round trip eats more than a quarter of its own R, and until now only the two RUNTIME doors enforced it (`paper.entry_cost_refusal`, `live_entry`) — `backtest_spec` opened on every matched signal. So every candidate's expectancy described a population the runtime would not trade: measured on this store, **48 of 50 15m entries refused at runtime, 11 of 17 at 1h, none at 4h**. **The gap is selection, not noise.** Cost in R is `fee_bps / stop_bps`, so what the door removes is systematically the tight-stop, low-ATR bars — the expensive ones — and scoring them into the mean answers a question nobody asked, pessimistically, at the fast end where the door removes almost everything. Matched comparison on real BTCUSDT bars, same specs and same window, door on against door off (10 specs per timeframe): **15m −0.4489 → −0.1863R** keeping 19% of trades, **1h −0.1335 → −0.0914R** keeping 72%, **4h +0.0048 → +0.0048R** keeping 100%. **This does not rescue 15m** — corrected, it is still clearly negative — but the −0.18R it is actually worth is a different number from the −0.45R the store was recording, and the decision this unblocks (whether to keep spending a third of the factory's daily capacity on 15m, 5 of 15 contexts) should rest on the first. The holdout runs the same door, because `holdout_status` gates ROBUST and a confirmation measured over a wider population would not be confirming the same thing. Recorded as its own `entry_cost_door` block rather than folded into `cost_basis`: the basis answers *"at what rates was this scored"* and its tier ordering refuses evidence scored more cheaply than the venue charges, while this is the same rates over a **narrower population** — folding it in would retier the whole store on a question the tier was not asked. Absence of the block is how a reader tells older evidence. Whether the promotion door should *require* it is deliberately not decided here (requiring it refuses the entire existing store at once; the convergence path is the same re-minting the cost basis already relies on). One trap named in the field's own comment: `refused_entries` counts refusal **events**, not opportunities — a refused signal leaves the book flat and can be refused again next bar while an accepted one occupies bars until it closes, so 672 trades without the door came with 3,613 refusals under it. It reads like a rejection rate and is wrong by an order of magnitude as one.
- Also done: **A schedule's cadence was anchored to whenever the tick happened to claim it, so being late made it permanently slower** (`scheduler.py`, `scheduler_cli.py`; 2026-08-02). Found by asking why `pm_scan`, registered at 120s, showed a **p50 gap of 140s over 2,264 fires** (p90 141, p99 154) — a steady 17% shortfall, not jitter. **Two independent causes, and neither alone produces it.** `claim_due` advanced `next_run_at` to `now + interval`, where `now` is the claim instant and therefore always a little after the due time; that lateness became the next anchor and compounded every cycle. And the tick loop slept a **fresh full interval after** the pass it had just run, so the poll grid was `work + interval` wide — a 20s scan on a 30s interval polled every 50s. Since a schedule can only be claimed on a poll line, the grid became the cadence, and each claim then landed later, which the first bug then made permanent. A simulation at pm_scan's own numbers (120s registered, 30s poll, 20s scan) reproduces **exactly 140s** on the old code and **exactly 120s** on the new: **617 → 720 readings per day, +16.7%**. That matters here rather than being cosmetic because the PM1 exit artifact's denominator is *readings* — the observation window was under-sampling itself by a sixth, and the window closes ~2026-08-10. **The fix is one concept in each half.** `next_occurrence(due_at, interval, now=…)` steps **whole intervals from the due time** until strictly past `now`, so lateness is per-occurrence jitter bounded by the poll period instead of accumulated drift; `remaining_period(interval, elapsed)` sleeps the period's **remainder**, clamped at zero. Both preserve what the module already guaranteed, and the preservation is the delicate part: stepping whole intervals past `now` means a scheduler down for a day **owes one occurrence, not 720** — the same "a kill drops the occurrence rather than queueing a burst" rule the kill path states — and "strictly past `now`" is what `claim_due`'s overlap guarantee and `schedule_run_id`'s no-collision argument both rest on. The clamp at zero is the matching promise on the loop side: a 190s `crypto_factory` fire polls again immediately and never borrows from the next period, because pacing polls is this function's job and *which* occurrences are owed is `claim_due`'s. **A third thing was wrong and is only visible once the first is fixed:** the failure alert told the operator `다음 실행: now + interval`, a second opinion that the store no longer agreed with, and it now recomputes the stored value. Tested at both scales — the exact-grid case (120s on a 30s poll: gaps are exactly 120), and the harder one where the cadence does not divide by the poll period (100s on 30s: every claim is late, no grid line makes it otherwise, and the long-run rate is still the registered one within a single poll period). The 30s tick interval in `docker-compose.yml` is unchanged: it sets the jitter bound, and after this it no longer sets the rate.
- Also done: **The `stop_atr` floor covered one of the factory's mint paths, and fusion carried children under it** (`crypto/factory.py`; 2026-08-02). Found by reading the promotable board an hour after #420 deployed: `GEN-738` sat on it at **`stop_atr` 1.1700**, below the floor that had shipped that morning. `fuse_specs` averages the parents' exit parameters and never consulted `_EXIT_PARAMS`. Averaging two values inside an interval lands inside it — a midpoint of a convex set is in the set — so the only escape is a **parent minted under an older space**, and 43% of the candidate store predates the floor moving 0.8 → 1.2. Measured on that day's mints: the direct path was clean **60/60**, fusion put **20 of 60** below the floor. **The first fix was wrong and a pre-existing test caught it.** `_EXIT_PARAMS` is strictly inside the validator's range (stop_atr [1.2, 2.0] against [0.3, 5.0]; target_atr [1.6, 8.0] against [0.5, 10.0]), so clamping unconditionally maps *every* value to a legal one — and an imported parent at `stop_atr` 12.0 was laundered into a valid child, deleting the refusal `fuse_specs` documents and `test_fusion_validates_the_child_even_when_a_parent_never_was` pins. **The clamp is a preference applied to legal inputs and must never rescue an illegal one:** an out-of-range parent now disables the clamp for that parameter, the midpoint goes through untouched, and `validate_strategy` refuses the child as before. Two legal parents, one merely older, clamp. The two bounds answer different questions — *is this a legal strategy* and *is this what the factory currently explores* — and only the second is a preference. Worth keeping as a shape: a bound that sits inside another bound cannot be enforced by clamping without silently widening the outer one. **~~Two mint paths are now covered and a third is not~~ — that claim was wrong and is corrected below.** It read: 40 candidates carry `created_by: RuleMinerAgent` (`MINE-662`…`MINE-811`, 32 below the floor), the string appears in no source file here or in the running image, its generation numbers interleave with the factory's — so *"which code may create a candidate"* had more than one answer. **Checked 2026-08-02 (#430): there are two mint paths, not three, and nothing outside this runtime writes to the candidate store.** The store's `provenance` field answers the question `created_by` was being read for, and it had the answer all along: the 41 rows at the head of the file are `crypto_ai_system_import` — the frozen crypto_AI_System, the same route the 114 imported paper outcomes came by, whose own agents were named `RuleMinerAgent` and `StrategyGenerationAgent` — and the 25 that reappear at lines 545–569 are `mvp_rescore`, this runtime re-scoring those imports on 2026-07-29 (`rescore_reason: "1d evidence re-scored at the MIN_FACTORY_BARS depth floor; the source candidate carried no cost model"`). A rescore preserves the source record's `created_by` and `generation_id`, which is what made the names look current and the numbering look interleaved. **Every one of the 450 rows after line 569 is `mvp_factory` or `mvp_factory_fusion`**, and the 32 below-floor mined rows froze before 2026-07-29 and cannot grow. The lesson is the one this file keeps recording in other forms: **`created_by` is the record's own account of itself and `provenance` is the store's account of the record** — reading the first as the second is how an import reads as an intruder.
- Also done: **One losing trade could demote a freshly promoted lineage, and the reason code named the wrong rule** (`crypto/lifecycle.py`; 2026-08-02). Found while asking whether the ladder's 20-trade window should be restated for 4h. **It should not, and no window size works:** the 4h ROBUST candidates run expectancy **+0.1183R against a per-trade sd of 1.402R**, so the zero threshold sits **0.38 SE** from the truth at 20 trades and **0.60** at 50 — a certified-robust strategy reads as losing **32.3%** of the time over a rolling 20 (worst 51.1%), and *shrinking* the window to 10 makes that **38.5%**. Pushing the threshold 2 SE out needs **562 trades ≈ 8.3 years at 4h**. What the search turned up instead is that the ladder already contains a far sharper instrument and it was the only unguarded one. `live_vs_backtest_win_rate_drop` reads **lifetime** trades rather than a rolling window, so `_full_window` never guarded it — and nothing else did: one losing trade puts the live win rate at 0.0 against a backtest 0.411, a drop of 0.411 past a 0.15 threshold. **Reproduced: `n=1 loss → PROBATION`.** The five lineages promoted 2026-07-31 escaped only because the first settlement was a win (SOLUSDT 4h SHORT, +1.778R). It compounds, too: PROBATION increments `consecutive_failures`, so by the time a rolling-50 window fills ~269 days later the counter would already exceed `suspend_consecutive`, and SUSPEND's "two consecutive failures" guard would be spent before it was ever needed. **The guard is 50 trades and the rule is worth guarding rather than weakening**, because a Bernoulli rate converges far faster than the mean of a heavy-tailed R distribution: at 50 trades this threshold sits **2.16 SE** from the truth where the rolling-50 expectancy test sits at 0.60, and its false-PROBATION rate on a strategy performing exactly to its backtest falls **31.6% at n=5 → 10.6% at n=20 → 1.9% at n=50**. It is the only rule that can decide something about a 4h lineage inside a horizon that lineage will actually reach. **A second defect fell out of the same lines:** both routes to PROBATION shared one reason list, so a demotion the win-rate rule caused also reported `rolling_30_below_probation_thresholds` — naming a window that was healthy and, below 30 trades, not even full. Each route now appends only its own reason. An unknown `trade_count` refuses the rule rather than waiving the guard: not knowing the sample is the state it reads worst, so it must not be the state in which it fires. No lineage in the current pool was affected — all five are `PAPER_ACTIVE` with `consecutive_failures: 0`.
- Also done: **The judgeability cap stopped hiding the operator's own pool** (`crypto/pool.py`; 2026-08-02). `MAX_DAYS_TO_LIFECYCLE_WINDOW` was 14, reproducing the retirement of 2026-07-30 (`approval_c1b8eeb7681c08579648`, 63 strategies) that kept one 15m lineage per symbol and retired everything slower — *"at current rates a 1d lineage needs 122d and a 1h lineage 29d to reach the 20-trade WARNING window, so neither can ever be auto-demoted."* **That value's premise was that 15m is the workhorse, and the cost model has since killed it:** at the current basis 15m nets **−0.1845R/trade** (friction 0.2768R against 0.0923R of gross edge) where 4h nets **+0.0889R**, so a cap admitting only 15m admitted only the timeframe that cannot pay for itself, and `promotable_backlog` read **0 with 900 candidates on file**. **What settles the new number is that the old one was hiding a decision the operator had already made.** Every one of the five lineages promoted 2026-07-31 sits above 14 days — 40.5, 50.4, 81.4, 107.7, 127.3 — so the board was refusing to advertise the exact class of thing being chosen, and reported an empty queue for a reason that had nothing to do with whether work was waiting. **130 is the slowest of those five, rounded up:** the anchor is the same KIND as the one it replaces (a recorded operator decision, read off what was actually done) and it is the decision that is newer. **The consequence is stated rather than smoothed over** — a lineage admitted at this horizon cannot be auto-demoted until it closes 20 trades, so retiring these is a manual act for months; that is not created here, it is already true of all five pool members, and hiding them did not make it less true. Still bounded: 1d's own fastest quartile is 341.5 days, 2.6× the cap, so the timeframe the original argument was written against stays excluded — and economics are **not** encoded here, because `promotable_backlog` already refuses on `expectancy_at_current_costs <= 0` per candidate upstream of this line. This cap answers *"can the runtime ever judge it"* and only that. Measured after: the board reports **2 promotable** where it reported 0, led by a `volatility_expansion_short` 4h lineage — the family minted specifically to widen 1R against a fixed-bps cost — with one 4h lineage still deferred at 162.8 days. The test fixtures moved with it (a 4h lineage at the store's median, 123 days, is now *inside* the cap and one at its 75th percentile, 259 days, is not), and a new test pins both bounds: at or above the horizon the operator promotes at, below 1d's fastest quartile.
- Also done: **The factory stopped minting into the two bands the cost model cannot pay for** (`crypto/factory.py`; 2026-08-02). Found by asking why `promotable_backlog` reads **0 with 900 candidates on file**: 660 are refused on cost basis (pre-funding mints, self-draining as the factory re-mints), 222 are not ROBUST, and the 18 that survive collapse to 10 lineages of which 7 are already promoted and 3 are deferred as unjudgeable. **The measurement underneath it is that gross edge is nearly flat across the ladder (+0.0923R at 15m, +0.1368R at 1h, +0.1517R at 4h) while cost varies 4.4× (0.2768R, 0.1553R, 0.0628R).** Signal quality is not what separates the timeframes; the denominator is — 1R is `stop_atr` × ATR and friction is a fixed ~10–16 bps. Decomposed, **taker fee plus slippage is 94% of all cost at every timeframe and funding is 0.2–4%**, which corrects the weight `REMAINING_WORK.md` puts on carry: that figure came from a 1d fixture holding 12–48 days, and on the 15m/1h/4h population that actually exists it rounds to nothing. **Two mint-time changes, both cheap and both second-order by admission.** `stop_atr`'s floor moves 0.8 → 1.2, where the store measures −0.3746R/trade at 15m below it against +0.0054R above (the aggregate overstates it — within families the gap is ~0.05–0.15R and runs the right way in 7 of 10, on cells of n=1–7 — so this stops wasting half of every mint on a band that is negative at 15m and worse at 4h than the alternative, and is not the fix for the fast end, where 0.28R of friction against 0.09R of gross edge is a 3× gap no stop multiple closes). **The base moves with the floor and that is not cosmetic:** `mutate_params` draws `base ± (hi − lo)·0.35` and clamps, so raising `lo` alone would pin **49.8%** of draws to exactly 1.2 — one value, one rule hash, a collapse in the parameter rather than a shift; at 1.45 it is 5.2% measured over 480 mints, with the tail still reaching 1.73 so the region the store has never sampled stays explorable. And `volatility_squeeze_*` is **retired** — 28 candidates over 14 generations, **zero ROBUST**, median **−0.2241R**, worst in the store at every timeframe. It reads as mechanism rather than luck, which is why one day of evidence is enough to stop minting on it: the pair gates entry on a LOW `bb_width_percentile`, a compressed band is a narrow ATR, and a narrow ATR is the small 1R every fixed-bps cost is divided by — its sibling `volatility_expansion_*` takes the other side of the same variable and is measurably cheaper (0.2438R vs 0.2713R at 15m). **Retired, not deleted:** the builders stay and `RETIRED_FAMILIES` records what would justify re-listing, because the premise ("a breakout is worth more out of compression") was never refuted — it was never given a risk unit large enough to be heard. Removing two families took the library 36 → 34 and with it `gcd(stride·count, total)` from 12 to 2, which collapsed the four-pass contrast in `test_a_context_covers_its_library_when_generation_ids_stride`; that test named this case in its own failure message, and the rewrite bounds **both** halves to one covering pass — "covers its library within one pass" is what the rotation cursor promises, "covers it eventually" is a coincidence of the library's size.
- Also done: **Every row this runtime mints counted as un-priceable, and Gate 0 could not have opened on any of them** (`crypto/feedback.py`; 2026-08-01). Found by measuring the first settlement of the newly promoted pool rather than by reading code: SOLUSDT 4h SHORT closed `take_profit` at **+1.778R**, `r_basis: intent_net_of_costs` — and the Gate 0 report read `costed_count: 0`, `uncostable_count: 1`, `expectancy: 0.0`, `failure_modes: [..., OUTCOME_NOT_COSTABLE]`. **`cost.outcome_net_r` returns `None` for an already-net row deliberately**, so a settlement that charged fees and slippage is never charged them twice; the defect is that `None` means two different things to a caller — *"this row cannot be priced"* and *"this row is already priced"* — and `summarize_net_of_costs` read both as the first. `paper.build_outcome_record` has labelled every row `intent_net_of_costs` since 2026-07-30, so **the entire forward record counted as uncostable**, and Gate 0 requires `not failure_modes`: that one mode would have held the live door shut however well the pool traded. **This is the third appearance of the same latch and the first on the runtime's own current rows** — imported history latched it the same way until #409 scoped the population, retired lineages latched it until #411 scoped it again, and this one no scoping could reach because the rows are the ones the gate is *supposed* to judge. **The fix charges the one cost that basis genuinely still owes, and no more.** What each basis contains had to be exact or a latch would have been traded for a wrong number: settlement charges fees and slippage and records no funding term, and `outcome_net_r`'s own docstring says the same — `intent_net_of_costs` is "fees and slippage both inside". Carry is therefore the remainder, and it is already this function's own responsibility (`net_result_r` owns carry precisely because deriving it needs `holding_candles × timeframe`). So net = `result_R` − carry, charged on the ENTRY FILL through `cost.funding_cost_r` — the same call `apply_cost_model` makes, not a second copy of the arithmetic. **The sign is the check that it is right rather than merely non-zero:** carry is signed, a long pays it and a short earns it, so this SHORT's net figure lands at **+1.813R, ABOVE** the stored +1.778R. A test pins short > stored > long, and pins that a zero-length hold passes the stored figure through untouched. **A row that owes carry it cannot price stays uncostable** — returning `result_R` there would report a figure silently missing a cost this function exists to charge. Measured after: `costed 1 / uncostable 0`, net expectancy **+1.813**, and the only remaining failure mode is `INSUFFICIENT_CLOSED_OUTCOME_SAMPLE` — 1 of the 20 the gate asks for, which is the honest answer.
- Also done: **The breaker watch started reporting the DOOR instead of one lock on it** (`crypto/breaker_watch.py`; 2026-08-01). Gate 0 became answerable by an operator acknowledgement the same day (#413), which introduced a way for the live door to shut that **nobody does**: an acknowledgement expires on its own window, and voids itself the instant the routable pool changes. A breaker trips because something happened; these happen because time passed or because the ladder demoted a strategy, so neither produces a moment anyone is watching — which makes them exactly the transitions an edge-triggered channel exists for. Without this, an operator finds out that live stopped entering from the *absence* of trades. **Reused rather than added:** this module already declared in its own docstring that what it speaks for is the LIVE entry gate, and Gate 0 is part of that gate, so extending it beat a second watch with a second marker, a second schedule kind and a second channel telling the operator about the same door. `evaluate` now also resolves the acknowledgement and the computed Gate 0 answer, and the state carries **both readings** — `gate0_eligible` (what the evidence says) beside `gate0_ack_applies` (what a person signed) — because a line showing only the effective answer could not tell a pool that earned the gate from one that was waved through it. **The headline reports `live_entry_open`, the two locks combined, and that is the point of the change**: an operator told "breaker released" while Gate 0 still refuses has been told something true and useless, and one told nothing at all when an acknowledgement lapsed has been told the most misleading thing available. `has_changed` gains the combined state, the acknowledgement's reason and the computed eligibility; it stays an EDGE trigger, and a test pins that settlements moving the numbers under an unchanged door still say nothing. **The reason is carried, not just the fact**, because `outside_validity_window` and `routable_pool_changed_since_signature` want different responses — one is re-signed, the other re-judged — and a message that only said "closed" would send an operator to the wrong one. Verified against the live machine, where an acknowledgement signed at 2026-08-01T10:26:36Z renders `gate 0 : evidence=False (sample 0) | operator=True` over `DOOR : live entries OPEN`; the delivery path was checked first rather than assumed (a real `TelegramChannel` and a registered chat, hourly `crypto_breaker_watch`), since a notification nobody receives is worse than none.
- Also done: **Gate 0 goes back to being answerable by the operator, without ceasing to be a gate** (`crypto/live_candidate_ack.py`, `schemas/crypto_live_candidate_ack.v0.1.schema.json`, `scripts/acknowledge_live_candidate_gate.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`; 2026-08-01). `CRYPTO_LIVE_EXECUTION_V0.1.md` states Gate 0 as an item on the **operator** go-live checklist — *"Paper trading by this runtime shows positive expectancy over a sustained window. Check with `python -m runtime.mvp_runtime.crypto.dashboard`"* — under a heading that reads *"Every step is Thomas's."* #400 found that `feedback.live_candidate_eligible` computed that judgement every cycle and that nothing read it, and #409 wired it into `live_entry` as refusal 2b. That closed a real gap and, in closing it, moved a checklist item from a person to a pool-wide aggregate — one number that refuses **every** strategy's entries for **any one** strategy's record. Measured 2026-08-01, the aggregate could not answer the question it was asked: all **86** rows holding the gate shut came from lineages `lifecycle` had already SUSPENDED and **zero** from one that could still route. #411 scoped the population, which fixes that defect and leaves the shape. **This does not remove the gate — `live_entry`'s refusal is untouched — it changes who may answer it**, in the idiom `risk_limits` and `live_budget` already use for the other checklist items: a self-hashed, expiring, per-machine record, written by one operator command. **Five properties, each a decision.** (1) **Nothing registered is today's behaviour exactly**; the override is opt-in and absent by default, which is what makes it safe to land at all. (2) **The measurement is never overwritten** — the report keeps computing `live_candidate_eligible` and the cycle record carries BOTH readings, what the evidence said and what the operator signed, because a record that hid the disagreement would be worse than no record. (3) **An acknowledgement is bound to the routable set it was signed for, by EXACT set equality** — promote a strategy or let the ladder demote one and it stops applying. A subset test would let a signature for two strategies authorise a pool that later gained a third nobody looked at, and it would do it silently, since the pool file changes without anything re-reading the record. Same auto-invalidation rule #405 states for the drawdown baseline, for the same reason. (4) **It expires**; a checklist tick is a judgement about a moment, and past the window the gate returns to its computed answer on its own. (5) **`resolve_ack` reports and never raises, and its failure direction is the opposite of `risk_limits`'** — there, an unusable record must refuse, because the thing it authorises is a looser breaker; here an unusable record simply does not apply, leaving the computed answer, which is the stricter of the two. Absent, expired, tampered, and signed-for-another-pool are four distinct reported reasons rather than one, because "nobody signed" and "somebody signed for a pool that no longer exists" want different responses from an operator. **The command is the deliverable, not the module.** `--show` prints the routable pool, what the evidence says and what has been signed, and it is deliberately placed BEFORE the foreign-root guard: that guard exists because a host-side root run leaves state the uid-10001 services cannot write, a read has no such consequence, and making the one command whose job is "look before you sign" refuse on a host shell would push an operator toward signing without looking. Signing requires `--registered-by` and `--reason` — a person overriding a measurement that says otherwise has to say on what basis — and refuses outright when the routable pool is empty, since there would be nothing to acknowledge. `--withdraw` returns the gate to its computed answer immediately. **What this does not do:** it grants no permission by itself. The `live_trading` grant, the confirmation phrase, the canary evidence, the registered budget, the loss breakers over live outcomes and both kill switches all still stand. What it *does* do is let live entries begin on the strategies it names, which is why the command prints them and why the record has to say who signed and why.
- Also done: **The loss breakers stopped metering a simulation and started metering the money** (`crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/guards.py`, `crypto/breaker_watch.py`; 2026-08-01). The half of #406 that Thomas deferred, and the one that actually fixes the problem the whole sequence was opened over. **`run_risk_guard` no longer reads the paper book.** It used to judge `own_outcomes + live_readable` — this runtime's paper rows plus its live ones — and live had traded nothing, so a real-money door was opened and closed entirely by a simulation. Measured on this machine 2026-07-31: the breaker read `weekly -19.35R` and `drawdown -44.79R` off **86 paper rows and 0 live ones**, and **757** ledger rows were HELD on it *while the router had a live entry candidate*, 749 of them on the weekly limit. Not one of those 86 rows had lost any money. **The three increments before this one did not touch that.** #407 took the PAPER leg off the breakers (a loss is evidence there, not damage, and the ladder is what judges a strategy); #409 wired Gate 0 into the live door; #410 built the drawdown-baseline rebase mechanism, which is inert until an operator registers a record. Each was real and none of them removed the paper→live coupling — they added a door and left the wrong one hanging. This removes it. **The symmetry is the argument**: `paper_trade_verdict` took the paper leg off the breakers because paper loses no money, and this takes the paper *evidence* off the live leg because it is evidence about a different book, at different size, with no cost of being wrong. A brake on real money has to answer *"is the money losing right now"*, and paper cannot answer that at any sample size. Each leg now meters what it actually risks. **The daily breaker was already live-specific and stays untouched** — `live_route` reads `venue_daily_realized_net(snapshot.realized_windows)`, the venue's own realized P&L against the registered 20 USDT limit, never `run_risk_guard`. So the scope here is exactly weekly, drawdown and consecutive-losses. **What now covers an unreadable PAPER store, which used to fail this guard closed:** Gate 0. `run_paper_performance_report` re-reads and raises, `report` is `None`, and `plan_live_entry` refuses a non-Mapping `live_candidate`. The live door still fails closed on that store — through the check that actually reads it rather than through a breaker that no longer does, which is one authority per question instead of two. An unreadable or tampered LIVE history still fails the breaker closed, unchanged. **A breaker over an empty history reads NORMAL, and that is the residual to keep in view.** `run_risk_guard`'s docstring has always said an empty list honestly means "no closed trades yet, which allows trading", and with live at zero rows that is now the ordinary case rather than a corner. So the verdict grew `judged_rows`: **"clear" and "clear because there is nothing to judge" are different states of a brake and only one of them is reassuring.** `breaker_watch` prints it as `rows: 0 live closed (judged) | 86 paper (not judged)` with `NOTE: no live outcomes yet - the loss breakers are INERT, not satisfied`. The paper count stands beside it deliberately: an operator who remembers a `-19.35R` weekly needs to see where that number went rather than watch it vanish between two reports. **Measured after the change, on the live machine: the C4 verdict is `ALLOW` and `plan_live_entry` still REFUSES, on `LIVE_ENTRY_NOT_LIVE_CANDIDATE`.** Gate 0 is what holds the door now — it needs **+43.43R** of net paper gain against the drawdown breaker's former +34.79R, so the door that replaced the coupling is stricter than the one removed. Beneath it the venue daily-loss breaker (20 USDT), the registered budget (75 USDT/order, 2/day, 120 USDT open) and the two-position cap all still bind at execution. **This also makes #405/#410's approval unnecessary for the live door** — the `-44.79R` it existed to re-base is no longer in the live verdict at all, so registering a rebase record becomes a question about a breaker that no longer reads those rows.  **And the same defect was in Gate 0, one layer up.** Scoping the breakers was not enough: `live_candidate_eligible` is measured over the paper record, so measured 2026-08-01 **all 86 rows holding it shut came from lineages `lifecycle` had already SUSPENDED and ZERO from one that could still route.** The ladder had done exactly its job — judge a strategy on its paper losses and retire it — and the losses of strategies that no longer exist were still gating a real-money door. That is #405's argument arriving by a second path: on a system built to rotate strategies, an all-time figure eventually measures elapsed time rather than performance, so every retirement leaves permanent dead weight in the mean and a pool that improved by exactly the route the design intends could never clear the gate it has to clear. `run_paper_performance_report` now takes `routable_strategy_ids` and judges only rows from a lineage that can still route. **Its `None` fails in the OPPOSITE direction to `guards.drawdown_baseline`'s, and that asymmetry is the point:** there, unknown routability must KEEP losses inside a brake; here it must WITHHOLD a claim that real money may start, so it scopes to nothing and Gate 0 refuses on `NO_OUTCOME_RECORDS`. Both refuse; neither guesses. The argument is **required, no default**, the `plan_live_entry` posture — an optional scope is one the caller that forgets it never applies, and the untested branch would then be the unscoped one. `domain_console`'s `_crypto_paper` scopes identically, because a console judging a different population would tell an operator the door is open on evidence the gate never read. **The threshold had to move with it, and that coupling was missed once before it was measured.** `MIN_SAMPLE_SIZE = 3` was harmless while the report covered 86 rows — three more cannot shift a mean over 86. Scoping to the routable pool makes it the BINDING constraint, and measured: **three profitable trades from a freshly promoted pool would take `live_candidate_eligible` from False to True** and open a real-money door. On a book running about -0.5R/trade net that is noise, so scoping the population without raising the floor would have been a relaxation wearing a defect fix's clothes — the two changes are one change. `LIVE_CANDIDATE_MIN_SAMPLE = 20`, **reused rather than invented**: it is `lifecycle.DEFAULT_WINDOWS[0]`, the window this repo already treats as "enough closed trades to judge a strategy's record" before it will even WARN one, and Gate 0 asks a strictly larger question — may this pool touch real money. A test pins the equality so the number cannot drift into an unsourced constant. `robustness.HEALTHY_TRADES_PER_PARAMETER` (10, "under ~5 it is noise") sits below it and agrees. Measured after: the live figure goes from `86 rows / NEGATIVE_EXPECTANCY_NET_OF_COSTS` to `0 rows / NO_OUTCOME_RECORDS` — still refusing, and now for the honest reason, since the pool that would trade has produced no evidence at all. **Nine tests changed rather than being deleted, and that is the specification moving**: every one of them seeded PAPER losses and asserted a breaker tripped. They now seed LIVE outcomes through `build_live_outcome_record`, so the fixtures are self-hashed and shaped exactly as `live_leg.execute_live_exit` writes them. Two new ones pin the change directly — a 20-row, -60R paper book that leaves the live verdict `ALLOW`, and an own-plus-imported fixture seeded so that BOTH failure directions would show (the own losses would trip the daily breaker if still counted, the imported profit would clear it if the split had been dropped instead of the store).
- Also done: **The paper leg stopped answering to a loss breaker — and the channel that announces the breaker had to say which leg it just stopped** (`crypto/guards.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/breaker_watch.py`; 2026-07-31). The design is `docs/proposals/PAPER_HAS_NO_LOSS_LIMIT_V0.1.md` (#406); this is its **paper half only**, which Thomas separated out precisely because it does not touch the live entry path — live stays exactly as blocked as it was while paper resumes. **A loss limit exists to stop money being lost, and paper loses none.** What metering paper against one actually did on this machine only showed up once it was counted: **86** own paper outcomes settled while **656** were refused by the loss breakers and recorded as counterfactual shadows — **7.6× more turned away than let through** — on consecutive-losses (394), weekly (393), daily (320) and drawdown (49). **The refusals were not the cost; the missing sample was.** The five strategies routing that day carried **2, 3, 3, 9 and 13** closed trades against `lifecycle` windows of warn 20 / probation 30 / suspend 50, so not one reached the lowest rung and the ladder — the thing that actually judges a strategy — could not demote any of them. The samples were thin *because* the breaker fired, and the breaker fired because those same strategies were losing: entries blocked → no sample accrues → the ladder cannot demote → the losing strategies stay routable → the breaker keeps firing. **A limit that suppresses the evidence that would clear it is a latch, not a brake**, and had the 656 settled every one of the five would have passed the suspend window weeks earlier. **So `cycle` computes two verdicts instead of one.** `paper_verdict` (`guards.paper_trade_verdict`) is data health alone — a cycle that cannot trust its candles must not simulate on them either, but a loss is *evidence* here rather than damage, and a portfolio-wide brake stops the good strategies to punish the bad ones and then destroys the sample that would have told them apart. `live_verdict` is `merge_trade_verdict`, **unchanged**, still folding in every breaker, and the live leg is handed that one — so a ledger row written before the split means exactly what a live-gating row means now, and nothing here widens real money. **The sentence this replaces held two guarantees and only one moved:** *"a live entry can never be permitted where a paper one was not"* conflated the SIGNAL ceiling (live only trades what paper's router proposed, via `shared_route`) with the RISK-STATE ceiling. The signal ceiling is untouched; the risk-state ceiling moved to a verdict that is *stricter* than the paper leg's rather than equal to it. **`risk_guard` is `None` on the paper verdict rather than a permissive stub**, which is the fail-closed shape and not a nicety: a caller reading breaker state off the paper leg is asking a question that leg no longer answers, and it should fail on the absence instead of being handed an "everything is clear" that was never evaluated. Nothing else on the paper leg is relaxed — the kill switch, the portfolio and per-symbol caps, the directional skew cap, `cost.MAX_ENTRY_COST_R` and the regime filter all still refuse — and the counterfactual book now keys on the paper verdict, so it stops shadowing refusals that no longer happen (those cycles produce a real settled row instead, which is strictly better evidence than the simulation of it) and keeps pricing what still refuses paper: data health, the economics gate, the caps. **The reporting half was the part that was nearly missed, and it is the more instructive failure.** `cycle_status_line` was correctly given a `live-gate=` token in the same change, so a trading-paper / refused-live cycle could not read as a contradiction — but `crypto_breaker_watch`, the one channel that speaks *when the breaker moves*, still rendered `CRYPTO BREAKER TRIPPED - new positions refused`. That wording was true while one merged verdict gated both legs and became false the moment paper stopped consulting it: the message an operator acts on would announce a stopped runtime that is, by design, still opening positions. **Worse than a stale label, because of how the watch decides to speak:** `has_changed` keys on the verdict, the verdict is now the live gate's, and the live gate does not clear when paper resumes — so the last thing the operator was told would have stood unchallenged, with no later fire to correct it. This is the `readiness_board` failure repeating through a different console, which is why the fix is a *standing* `scope` line rather than a one-off word change. **What that channel now also reports is the honest shape of the open half:** the live gate reads `weekly -19.35R` and `drawdown -44.79R` off **86 paper rows and 0 live ones** (measured 2026-07-31), so every figure in a real-money breaker's message is currently a simulation's. That is the intended design — paper evidence is what says whether live may START — but it reads exactly like a live figure, so it is printed as `86 paper + 0 live closed` with the note naming it, reported and never keyed on (settlements move it constantly, and a watch that fired on them is the trade feed this was built not to be). **What was deliberately NOT built, and the shortcut that must not be taken instead.** #406 §6's live half — rebuilding the weekly, drawdown and consecutive breakers over LIVE outcomes, only the daily one being live-specific today — is ordered **after** #400 (Gate 0 wiring), because with live's own breakers starting from an empty history Gate 0 is what governs whether live may start at all. Doing it now would take live's weekly to `0.00R` and its drawdown to `0.00R`, which is a **loosening of the real-money door** and needs its own approval rather than arriving as a consequence of a paper change. The tempting shortcut is to re-base the window by DATE — "the legs are split now, so count from today" — and it is the wrong instrument for three reasons: it reaches the same `0.00R` without stating a rule, so it has to be re-argued every time; #405's closing rule (preserved when it was closed into #406) already governs re-basing and is **lineage**-based, requiring an outcome to prove it came from a lineage that is not currently routable, with unattributable rows never leaving; and it is not even needed for the weekly figure, which is a rolling calendar-week window that self-clears at **2026-08-03T00:00Z**. The drawdown is the one that persists — `_drawdowns_r` measures the CURRENT distance from the equity peak over the whole history, so it unlatches on a new peak and never on a date.
- Also done: **The positioning store's features, wired — with the flip handed to the data rather than to a future reader** (`crypto/features.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/factory.py`, `scheduler.py`; 2026-07-30). The last open item from `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, and the one that had been deferred with "collect now, decide later". **A test I wrote when the store shipped fired on exactly this change** — `test_no_feature_column_reads_the_store`, whose stated job was to turn red the day someone wired it and make them read the module docstring first. It did its job, and reading it changed the SHAPE of the wiring rather than the decision: the columns are honest at any coverage (absent = None, the `open_interest` posture), so attaching them is safe today; what is not safe is MINTING a family against a window the store cannot cover. So the tripwire moved rather than being deleted — it now guards the minting gate, which is where the hazard actually lives. **The hazard, stated precisely, because the earlier note had it roughly:** the vendor keeps 30 days and the factory replays 500, and a family minted over a 6%-covered window would not merely be *thin* — it would be **un-scoreable**. Every trade would land in the newest walk-forward slice and none in the older ones, so `temporal_consistency` is 0 by construction and no amount of real edge could clear the robustness bar; the family would then be retired as FRAGILE and blamed for a window that had no data in it. That is `liquidation_spike_ratio` in a different costume. **So the gate is the increment.** `POSITIONING_FAMILIES` mint only when `positioning_store.coverage_summary(...)["eligible"]` says the store spans the replay window — the store's own measurement against its own `REQUIRED_COVERAGE_DAYS = FACTORY_DEPTH_DAYS`, so no second authority on how much history a feature needs. Everything else is built and tested now, and the data flips it on: nobody has to remember a paragraph in a document sixteen months from now. `templates_for_timeframe` takes `positioning_eligible` as a PARAMETER because it is pure and coverage lives on disk; the scheduler's factory path reads the store. Its default is **False**, deliberately the opposite of the `symbol=None` carve-out beside it: an unstated symbol is a question about the library, but unstated coverage is a caller who did not measure, and guessing wrong there mints the un-scoreable family. **The columns.** `positioning_divergence` is `top_position` (the top cohort's POSITIONS) minus `global_account` (every account) — the research record's specific pair, and the only measurement in this module OHLCV cannot reproduce at any resolution: every other source here, taker flow included, is a property of transactions, while this is a property of what is HELD. `top_account` is collected and is deliberately neither leg — it counts heads inside the same cohort, so pairing it with `global_account` would compare headcount to headcount and measure nothing about capital; a test pins that. **Mintable: only the standardised pair** (`positioning_divergence_zscore`, `positioning_divergence_change`). The divergence LEVEL and the three raw shares are evidence only — dimensionless is not the same as comparable, and whether +0.05 means the same on BTC as on DOGE is a question nobody has answered, so minting a threshold on it would assert the answer. The `xs_dispersion` split exactly. **Alignment is backward as-of** (`_asof_align`), the funding and open-interest rule and now the module's fourth consumer of a third distinct grid rule: the store is HOURLY on its own cadence, so a 15m bar at :15 carries the reading at :00. The exact join used by the derivative-price and cross-section legs would blank three bars in four here, and as-of over there would fabricate a basis for a bar the venue skipped — same module, three grids, three rules, each tested where it applies. **The derived values are computed in the SERIES' own time base and aligned afterwards**, which is the `open_interest` lesson repeated rather than rediscovered: a diff of the aligned column is zero on every bar between two hourly readings, and a z-score over it would mean 100 bars rather than 100 observations. Four mutations turn the new tests red — the exact join, pairing `top_account` instead of `top_position`, the gate defaulting open, and the change measured over bars. **Measured on a seeded store:** 720 rows across three series align onto 240 hourly bars, the divergence reads exactly `top − crowd`, the z-score is determinate on 200/240 bars, and a 0.15 swing shows in `positioning_divergence_change` at exactly +0.150. **No claim of edge:** there is no history to make one on, which is the entire point of the gate.
- Also done: **What a position cap costs, made measurable — and the global-entry arbiter deliberately NOT built** (`crypto/cycle.py`; 2026-07-30). Asked for the globally-best entry: the two-phase fan-out that evaluates every context before executing any, which both the directional cap below and #352's ordering change stopped at. **Measured first, and the measurement said do not build it.** In the shipped pool shape — one routable strategy per context (#364), twenty contexts, 1,200 bars — **no cap ever bound**: zero declines from the portfolio count, the per-symbol count or the directional lean; only 3 bars out of 1,200 had more than one proposal at all; and zero bars had a cap decline while another proposal competed. **There is no contention to arbitrate.** Worse for the idea, the specific defect an arbiter would fix — a context ranked by a strategy that does not fire taking the slot with a weaker one that does — needs several strategies per context, which `MAX_ROUTABLE_PER_CONTEXT = 1` now forbids. #364 closed it. And unlike every increment before it on this branch, an arbiter is **not one-directional**: it reallocates rather than declines, so it cannot inherit the "a gate that only reduces trading needs no gate of its own" argument and would need its own justification, which the measurement does not supply — against a restructure of the most safety-critical function in the stack (splitting settle-from-open inside `run_paper_update`'s portfolio lock, reassembling the cycle record across two phases, re-deriving `live_halt`). **That measurement also revises a claim made one increment earlier:** the directional cap's benefit numbers (16.3% → 2.3% of time over the cap, 57 declines) came from a simulation that gave every context the WHOLE 32-family library — a pool #364 forbids. In the shipped shape the cap currently never binds. It is not wrong and it is not wasted; it is insurance for a pool that grows or a book that fills, which is a fine thing for a safety cap to be. But the honest statement is "has not bound yet", not the measured benefit that PR quoted. **What shipped instead is the thing that turns the question into evidence.** The counterfactual book (C11) has always shadowed entries the C4 GUARDS refused, so "what did the daily-loss breaker cost" is answerable from the ledger; the three POSITION caps had no such record, which is exactly why the paragraph above had to reason from mock candles. A cap refusal now opens a shadow tagged with the cap's **own** reason code, so `counterfactual_by_reason` prices each cap on the machine that ran it. Purely observational — shadows never trade — and it reuses the mechanism rather than inventing measurement, the `positioning_store` "accumulate now, decide later" posture. **One property makes it cheap, and it is not shared by the branch beside it:** `open_refused` is set only INSIDE the freshness gate, so a cap shadow opens once per refused candle, where a guard-blocked shadow opens once per TICK because a tripped breaker persists across every tick of a coarse timeframe. The two branches are mutually exclusive by construction — `run_paper_update` only reaches its cap checks when the verdict already allows a new position — and a test pins that a cap reason never leaks into a guard-blocked shadow. Three mutations turn the new tests red; a fourth did **not**, which is recorded rather than hidden: the `opened is None` clause is redundant by construction (a cycle that opened had an allowing verdict and no refusal), so removing it alone changes no behaviour. It is kept because the invariant it states — a trade that HAPPENED is never also shadowed, or it is double-counted into every bucket — is what a future third branch would break silently, and the test pins the behaviour rather than the line. **When the arbiter would become worth building:** when this record shows cap refusals with materially positive counterfactual R, i.e. real contention with a real cost. That is a decision for evidence from the live machine, not for a simulation — which is the whole point of shipping the measurement instead of the mechanism.
- Also done: **A directional cap on the paper portfolio — the third cap, and the first that reads the book's shape rather than its size** (`crypto/paper.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/dashboard.py`; 2026-07-30). The portfolio-level authority the cross-sectional entry below left explicitly open, shipped as **the part that can be one-directional**. `MAX_CONCURRENT_POSITIONS` (20) and `MAX_POSITIONS_PER_SYMBOL` (4) bound how *many* positions exist and how many share a symbol; **neither bounded how many point the same way**, so twenty simultaneous longs were permitted — the F1 hazard `docs/TRADING_STRATEGY_REVIEW_RECORD.md` names, where one market move becomes twenty correlated verdicts about twenty different strategies. `paper.directional_skew_admits` is a third refusal inside the SAME portfolio lock, counted from the `open_books` the two existing caps already had in hand — no new chokepoint, no extra read, and the lock is what stops two cycles from each counting before either wrote. **What was deliberately NOT built, and why that is the whole shape of this increment:** balancing k long against k short requires *creating* the opposing position, which is the runtime inventing an entry no strategy proposed — a new authority over money and a Thomas decision. What shipped can only ever **decline**, which is the `regime_admits` / `vol_size_multiplier` posture and the reason it needs no gate of its own.
