So this is exposure **discipline**, not enforced market neutrality; the difference is stated in the research record rather than blurred. **The cap is derived, not chosen:** it IS `MAX_POSITIONS_PER_SYMBOL`, so the rule has an English statement with no number in it — *the portfolio's net directional bet may never exceed one symbol's full allocation.* And it is provably **not a concurrency cap in disguise**, which is what makes the derived value right rather than merely tidy: at 20 slots a book of 12 long + 8 short is net 4, so the full portfolio limit stays reachable and what is forbidden is a full book that is *one-way*. A test pins exactly that, because a cap that silently shrank the portfolio would look identical from outside. **The sign convention is load-bearing and a reader will want to "simplify" it back:** everything is counted *toward the proposal* (`aligned`, `opposing`, lean = `aligned - opposing`), so the rule is one comparison and the corrective-safe property falls out for free — a lean that would exceed the cap is only reachable from a book that already leans that way, so an entry that *reduces* an imbalance is never declined. Written as `abs(new_skew) > cap` it looks equivalent and is not: once unsynchronised exits have pushed the book past the cap on its own, that form refuses the very trades that would rebalance it. **Measured, on a 1,200-bar five-symbol twenty-context simulation driven by the shipped function:** the absolute form blocked corrective entries for no gain (identical opened count, identical worst-case lean), and the shipped rule cut the time the book spent leaning more than one symbol's worth from **16.3% to 2.3%** and the worst observed lean from **8 to 6**, for **2.9% fewer positions opened** (452 → 439) and a mean book size of 8.6 → 8.3. Worth stating that the no-cap worst case was 8 against a mean book size of 8.6 — **at times the entire book was one direction**, on five *independent* random walks; the mock's own `ref_correlation` runs ±0.05 between symbols where real majors run +0.7..+0.9, so every number here is a **floor**, not an estimate. **The limit is read from the module global at call time and is deliberately not a parameter**, matching its two siblings — a `cap=MAX_DIRECTIONAL_SKEW` default argument binds the number at import, and that is not hypothetical: it is what the first version did, and the end-to-end test caught it opening a position the cap was set to refuse. **An entry-time gate cannot bound the book thereafter, and the alternative is worse:** exits are unsynchronised, so a balanced 12/8 book becomes 12/0 when the shorts time out — which is why the honest claim is "the skew this runtime deliberately takes on", and why the book still sits over the cap 2.3% of the time. Bounding it after the fact would mean closing positions the strategies did not close: a new authority over exits that would falsify the outcome record paper exists to produce. **Live is untouched, and not by omission** — at `live_position.MAX_LIVE_CONCURRENT_POSITIONS = 2` the live book's largest possible lean is 2, so a cap there is either inert or halves an already-tiny capacity, and live one-way risk is already governed by the operator-registered `max_open_notional_usdt`: that authority exists and is Thomas's number, not one this runtime should second-guess. A test is the tripwire rather than the comment — raise live concurrency past the paper cap and it fails, which is exactly when someone must decide whether live needs its own answer. **Observability was a pre-existing gap this widened and therefore fixed:** `open_refused` reached the ledger only inside `paper_records`' event stream and never the status line, so both count caps have been invisible to anyone reading a fire — tolerable while they fired at twenty positions, not once a third cap can decline a *half-full* book. It is now on the cycle record and the status line with the book's shape attached (`refused=...(LONG 4v0 lean=5>4)`), because "the book leans 5 against a limit of 4" is the actionable part and a bare reason code is not. And the lean is on the **dashboard**, derived at read time and never stored — the `pool.candidate_quality` rule, sharpened here because `MAX_DIRECTIONAL_SKEW` derives from a constant that has already moved once. It belongs on the board and not only on the per-fire line because the gate declines on *standing* state: an operator who missed the one fire that printed a refusal still needs to see the book is one-way. Positions whose direction is neither LONG nor SHORT are **counted, not dropped**, on the board and in the gate — the gate treats them as aligned with whatever is proposed (so a corrupted record makes the cap bind *sooner*, the `list_open_positions` failure direction), and a board that omitted them would show a book more balanced than the one the gate is judging. One more thing the tests caught rather than the author: `render_status_text` is handed status dicts built by older builds and by hand, so gating the new line on the FIELD rather than on `open_positions` is what keeps the board from crashing on exactly the history an operator opens it to read. **A sixth mid-flight `main` merge added a required half to this**, and it is the clearest semantic conflict this branch has hit: #364 landed `MAX_ROUTABLE_PER_CONTEXT = 1`, so every context now holds exactly one strategy and a spec's `direction` is fixed at PROMOTION time — which moves "which directions can the book hold" from a property of the template library (balanced 16 long / 16 short, which is what the simulation above assumed) to a property of the POOL, which nothing balances. There was no textual conflict and every test passed. But the claim this increment is sold on — *not a concurrency cap in disguise* — became conditional: measured after the merge, a pool of twenty long strategies can fill **four** of its twenty slots, and one of 18/2 can fill eight. `pool.routable_directional_capacity` reports it (`2 * min(long, short) + cap`, the cap's own arithmetic, so no second number), on the board beside the lean, on stdout at the promotion door, and on the promotion LEDGER record because that promotion is the moment the composition was decided. **Reported, not refused, and the boundary is deliberate**: the directional cap can only decline, so a lopsided pool trades less rather than unsafely — turning it into a fifth promotion guard would add a blocking authority over the operator's pool for a non-safety concern, and would forbid assembling a pool in any order but alternating, since five longs before the first short is ordinary. The tests caught a real bug in that reporter within a minute of being written: `spec.direction` is a `Direction` **enum**, so `str(...).upper()` yields `"DIRECTION.SHORT"`, matches neither bucket, and reports every pool as perfectly one-way — it now normalises exactly as `strategy.evaluate_spec` does. **A seventh merge, and this one improved the increment rather than threatening it:** #352 replaced the fan-out's alphabetical visit order with tiers (live-holding contexts, then paper-holding, then best `champion_score` descending). This cap is evaluated per context as the fan-out reaches it — like the two beside it — so the room under it goes to whoever asks first, and "first" had meant *alphabetically*: the last directional slot would have been an accident of spelling, the same tiebreak-by-alphabet hazard rejected in the cross-sectional rank. It now goes to the best-evidenced context. Neither change owns that property alone, so a test pins the composition and a mutation reverting the sort turns it red. What it still does not promise is the *globally best* entry — a score is not a signal, so the top-scoring context may propose nothing this bar — and #352's own message names the same boundary this increment ran into: arbitrating properly needs every context evaluated before any executes, which is a second pass over the fan-out and a decision of its own.
- Also done: **Cross-sectional context — the first feature whose value depends on symbols the cycle is not trading** (`crypto/market_data.py`, `crypto/features.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/factory.py`, `scheduler.py`; 2026-07-30). Item 8 / T3-3 of `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, the last on that list and the one it called "biggest expected value, biggest change". **It shipped as half of what the note described, and the half is named rather than quietly redefined.** The note listed three blockers — `route_entries` is single-symbol premised, `symbol_scope` is effectively single-symbol, the position book is (symbol, timeframe) keyed — and **none of them is touched here.** Instead the cohort rank became a *feature*: `xs_rank_pct`, `xs_excess_roc_4`, `xs_dispersion_ratio` (mintable) plus `xs_dispersion` and `xs_members` (evidence), so "long the top k, short the bottom k" is expressible in the existing declarative spec language, scored by the existing backtest, and judged by the existing robustness gate. **No new authority over entries exists, which is the reason to prefer this shape** — the alternative was a portfolio layer that overrides what specs propose, and that is a governance change, not a feature. **What is therefore NOT delivered:** the note claimed market beta "structurally cancels", and that cancellation only happens while longs and shorts are held *simultaneously*. Here it happens probabilistically, when both families fire — it is not enforced. Saying so is the point; a reader who assumed the structural version shipped would be wrong about the runtime's exposure. **`rel_strength_roc_4` could not have substituted, and the reason is worth keeping:** it measures against ONE proxy, so every altcoin can beat BTC in the same hour and the column cannot say which of them to buy — and it says nothing at all about which to sell. A rank answers both. **The cohort is a declared constant (`CROSS_SECTION_UNIVERSE`, six large-cap perps), not "whatever the pool routes today"**, and that is the load-bearing decision: a rank is a statement *about a cohort*, so a cohort that followed the pool would make `xs_rank_pct >= 0.8` mean "strongest fifth of six" at mint time and "strongest fifth of three" after two demotions — same threshold, different claim, nothing in the record showing the substitution. Worse, the cohort would then depend on the pool, which depends on the backtests, which were scored against the cohort. Editing the tuple invalidates every `xs_*` backtest mined under the old cohort, exactly as editing `ROC_FAST` does; `xs_members` rides on the row so a record shows what its rank was actually computed over. **Six members, chosen by arithmetic:** `xs_rank_pct` has only `members - 1` distinct values, so three symbols offer {0, 0.5, 1.0} and a mined threshold is a choice among three buckets — and twenty would be twenty candle series per timeframe per fan-out. `MIN_CROSS_SECTION_MEMBERS = 4` is the floor below which no rank is reported at all, because a two-member rank is a coin flip wearing a six-member column name. **Degradation is per peer, and that is a deliberate divergence from `attach_reference`**: there one series either arrived or the leg was indeterminate; here five of six is a usable cohort, and refusing to rank because the sixth timed out throws away a measurement the runtime has. So the cohort thins, `xs_members` records how many answered, and `CROSS_SECTION_DEGRADED` fires when any peer failed — visible in the record rather than only in a column nobody reads. **Peer momentum comes from `indicators.roc`, not from the full pipeline, and that is what makes six members affordable**: `_reference_columns` recurses into `build_feature_rows` because it needs `ref_market_regime` (a classifier over many columns), but the only peer quantity wanted here is the same `roc` call `build_feature_rows` itself makes — so it is the same authority, not a second one, and it is six `roc` calls instead of six 25-indicator pipelines over a window that is 12,000 bars deep in the factory. Alignment is the **exact open-time join** via `_same_grid_column` — the fourth consumer of that rule — because peers are requested AT this frame's interval; a bar the venue skipped drops that peer rather than carrying its previous value forward, which would rank this symbol against a stale reading. **`ReferenceCandleCache` became `PeerCandleCache`**, generalized by one `symbol` argument rather than duplicated, because the concept is "one candle read per (symbol, timeframe, depth) per fan-out" and a cached read has no opinion about which leg wanted it. That generalization is not cosmetic: this leg is the largest redundancy surface in the runtime — measured on the shipped 5x4 grid, **116 peer reads without the cache and 24 with it**, against a pre-change baseline of 4. Both legs therefore share one default depth (240): a different default would make the proxy's series a cache MISS between the two legs, two reads for one answer. **The 24 still overlap the fan-out's own 20 context reads and that is left alone on purpose** — routing a context's collection through a cache would put its provenance record behind a cache, and the ledger keeps that record to prove what data a cycle saw. Net cost is +20 klines reads per 15-minute fire, well inside the venue's weight budget. **The long and short legs share one `xs_rank_edge` parameter capped below 0.5, and that is a safety property rather than tidiness:** long requires `rank >= 1 - edge`, short requires `rank <= edge`, so the two are DISJOINT across the whole param space. Independent floor/ceiling params could be mined into an overlap (long >= 0.4 while short <= 0.6), and a long and a short matching on one feature row is `paper.BLOCK_DIRECTION_CONFLICT` — the pair failing closed on exactly the bars it was minted for. A test asserts the property of the SPACE, not of one mined value. **`xs_dispersion_ratio` is self-calibrating for the `atr_pct_reference` reason** — a dispersion LEVEL would be a number nobody authorized and would mean one thing at 1h and another at 4h, so the mintable column is a ratio to its own rolling median (measured median 1.000, range 0.317-2.062 on a 400-bar mock frame), and `ParamSpec(0.8, 1.6)` is well-scaled by construction rather than by a guess. `CROSS_SECTION_FAMILIES` gates on cohort size and **does not bind today**, which is why it needs a test: shrink the universe below the floor and every `xs_*` column is permanently None, so the families would still mint, still backtest, take zero trades, and be retired as FRAGILE — the family blamed for a cohort too small to rank. Unlike htf_* and session_*, nothing about a coarser bar makes a rank undefined, so these survive at 1d, where the library is otherwise thinnest (now 32 families at 1h, 26 at 1d). **Measured, not asserted:** on mock candles the rank is near-uniform across its six buckets and determinate on 296/300 bars, both legs fire on ~16% of 2,000 bars, and a 6,000-bar backtest closes 207 trades across all six regimes. **No claim of edge, same as every entry above it:** that backtest lands PROVISIONAL with negative expectancy, which is the correct verdict for a hash-derived random walk and says only that the families are reachable and scoreable.
- Also done: **Volatility-scaled live sizing — the risk taken was rising with volatility** (`crypto/paper.py`, `crypto/live_sizing.py`, `crypto/features.py`, `crypto/indicators.py`; 2026-07-30). Item 7 of `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, and the note's own prescription was wrong in a way its own caveat predicted. It said to make `risk_fraction` a function of volatility, and warned that the budget cap might bind first. Measured: the cap binds for essentially every realistic account — at a 60 USDT per-order cap the risk leg only wins below roughly **30 USDT of equity** — so scaling `risk_fraction` is **provably inert**, which is precisely the "looks like it does something" outcome this repo keeps catching. **The measurement then found something worse than inertness.** While the cap binds the notional is FIXED, so the risk actually taken is `cap × stop_distance / price` and therefore **rises with volatility**: across plausible stop distances at one cap, 0.18 → 1.20 USDT, a factor of **6.7**, biggest bets on the most chaotic tape. That is the opposite of volatility targeting, and it was the live behaviour. So the multiplier applies to the **final quantity**, after `min(risk, budget)` — which is what makes it work through whichever leg bound. Verified: risk taken goes from 0.600/1.200 USDT at 2x/4x normal volatility to a **constant 0.300**, equal to what the reference volatility itself takes. **Capped at 1.0, and the name says so.** `vol_size_multiplier` can only shrink an order. Letting it exceed 1.0 would size above the operator's registered per-order cap in quiet markets — widening an authorization from inside the runtime, which is a Thomas decision and not a multiplier's business. So this is a volatility-scaled **ceiling**, not a two-sided target: in unusually quiet conditions it leaves size where the cap already put it rather than reaching for a risk target. Same one-directional posture as the regime gate, for the same reason. **No target-volatility constant exists**, deliberately: the ratio is `atr_pct_reference / atr_pct_of_price`, both measured from the same series, so there is no number anybody failed to authorize (the #356 lesson) and none that would mean one thing on BTC and another on DOGE. The reference is a **rolling median**, not a mean, and the direction of that error is the argument: its consumer *divides* by it, so a reference dragged upward by the very spikes it should react to yields a LARGER multiplier — less de-risking, the unsafe direction. `indicators.rolling_median` is a non-port like `rolling_correlation` and says so. **A limitation stated rather than hidden:** the floor (0.25) means past 4x the reference volatility the reduction stops tracking — risk is 4x smaller than it would have been, not constant, and a test pins exactly that rather than implying constancy everywhere. Below the floor the venue's own minimum quantity and notional refuse the entry, which is where a refusal on a chaotic tape should come from. **What was investigated and deliberately NOT changed:** live sizing uses `live_sizing.RISK_PER_TRADE_FRACTION` rather than the operator-registered `risk_limits.risk_per_trade`, which looked at first like #356's defect in the one place that moves money. It is not — the constant's own docstring already makes the argument (the fraction can only ever produce a size *below* the registered USDT caps, so it cannot widen an authorization, and making it tunable is a schema bump), and `risk_limits.risk_per_trade` has a different job: converting `max_drawdown_pct` into an R limit for the C4 breaker. Wiring one into the other would make a single number serve two authorities. Paper is untouched by construction, not by omission: its accounting is R-based and R is normalized by risk, so no quantity exists there for a multiplier to scale — this is a live-path change, and the multiplier rides on the plan (like `max_holding_bars`) so the execution step reads it rather than re-deriving a number the two could disagree about. `vol_size_multiplier` is recorded on every sizing record including refusals and including 1.0, because "the scaler did nothing" and "there was no scaler" are different statements and the second is what every record before this said.
- Also done: **Regime-conditional routing — decline a regime a strategy has demonstrated it loses in** (`crypto/paper.py`, `crypto/factory.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `scripts/promote_strategy_candidates.py`; 2026-07-30). Item 6 of `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, and it was not the change that document predicted. The note said the evidence was already there — *"`backtest_evidence.regime_breakdown` already computes per-regime R totals, so the routing just has to read it"* — and **both halves of that were wrong**: `backtest_spec` computed `regime_r` per regime and then collapsed it to `sorted(regime_r)` plus a profitable-count, throwing away the only thing that says *where* the edge was; and the pool entry carried `champion_score`, `candidate_id`, `strategy_rule_hash`, `generation_id` and the spec, but **no evidence at all**, so the router had nothing to consult even if it had been kept. So the increment is three things: preserve `per_regime` on the evidence, copy it onto the pool entry at promotion, and gate routing on it. **Why the pool entry rather than a candidate-store lookup:** the router reads the pool, and making it resolve `candidate_id` against the candidate store would put a 359-row store whose reader deliberately **raises** on damage — because it backs a promotion an operator signs — on the path of every 15-minute cycle, so one corrupt line would stop routing. `champion_score` is already on the entry for exactly this reason; this follows it. **The raw numbers, never a derived exclusion list**, and that is a scar this repo already carries: `pool.candidate_quality` had to stop reading stored robustness verdicts because they were written once at mint time, the rule that produced them changed, and twelve candidates kept a label the new rule could not produce — inverting the shortlist on precisely the property the new rule existed to enforce. A baked `excluded: [...]` would repeat that the first time the threshold moved, so `paper.regime_admits` owns the rule and applies it at read time. **The gate's whole design is the direction it fails in.** It can only ever make the pool trade *less* — it never admits an entry the existing checks refuse, which is why it needs no gate of its own — and the cost of a wrong exclusion is **silent**, since no record shows the trades that did not happen. So every uncertain case admits: no `regime_evidence` (every strategy promoted before this), a regime the replay never traded, a malformed cell, an unreadable regime label. Only "traded enough, and lost" declines. `MIN_REGIME_TRADES_TO_EXCLUDE = 10` reuses `robustness.HEALTHY_TRADES_PER_PARAMETER`'s stated line for "enough observations to mean anything" rather than inventing a number, and the clause matters more than the value: slicing an outcome history by regime multiplies the comparisons made on it, which is the overfitting hazard `docs/TRADING_STRATEGY_REVIEW_RECORD.md` raises about this pipeline generally — a per-regime sign read off three trades would be that hazard wired into live routing. Deliberately **not** registered in `crypto_risk_limits`: that schema owns the five C4 breaker limits, which bound money loss and therefore need authorization (#356's point); a filter that only ever trades less is a different concept and one authority per concept. **The filter runs on a MATCH, not before evaluation**, so the record still shows the rules fired and says separately that the regime declined — filtering earlier would make an excluded strategy indistinguishable from one whose conditions were simply not met, and those want different responses. Same reason `regime_excluded_strategy_ids` is named on the route and `regime_excluded` on the cycle record beside `route_status`: a cycle that entered nothing because every match was regime-excluded otherwise reads identically to one where the market offered no setup. The record carries the **ids**, not a count, because a strategy excluded on every cycle for a week is a demotion candidate and a count cannot say which one; the status line prints it only when it happened, for the reason the live leg does — a field empty on almost every line trains the reader to skip it. Backward-compatible by construction and tested as such: a pool promoted before this routes byte-identically, and `run_promotion` on a candidate whose evidence predates `per_regime` installs `regime_evidence: None`. **One interaction found by merging `main`:** #359 had just landed because a SUSPENDED entry could not say why it stopped trading, and this increment creates a *new* way to stop trading that is **not a status at all** — a regime-excluded entry stays `PAPER_ACTIVE` and keeps its routing slot, so nothing in the pool says it is inert. That is the same blind spot arriving by another path, so the board counts exclusions over the cycle records it already reads, beside #359's demotion-reason line, as a **share** of cycles read: `3/12` is the filter working and `12/12` is a strategy that has stopped working here, and a bare count cannot tell those apart. **And #360, merged the same hour, asks the question this gate has to answer about itself:** it landed because the backlog counted candidates whose forward verdict was unreachable — at current rates a 1h lineage needs 29 days to reach a 20-trade window and a 1d lineage 122 — so "is there enough evidence for this rule to ever fire" is now a standing question, not a nicety. Measured rather than assumed, on a 4,000-bar mock replay across all 30 families: **15 have at least one regime slice at or above the 10-trade bar, and 13 produce at least one exclusion.** So the gate bites on roughly 43% of the library rather than being decorative — and it is reachable for the right reason, because it reads BACKTEST trades (a 12,000-bar replay) rather than live ones, which is exactly the depth #360 found live lineages lack. The families with no adequate slice are the ones that close almost nothing on the mock (htf_*, oi_* with no feeds configured, bollinger_breakout, mean_reversion), which is a mock artefact rather than a limit of the rule. The honest caveat on the same number: a hash-derived random walk makes *every* strategy lose in some regime, so this measures that the gate has evidence to act on — not that acting on it improves returns. That is a question for real replay evidence, like every other claim in this pair of increments.
- Also done: **Positioning history the runtime retains itself — collect now, decide later** (`crypto/positioning_store.py`, `crypto/market_data.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/dashboard.py`; 2026-07-30). Tier 2 of `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, and the first increment here whose honest description is that **it makes no money today and is worth doing anyway**. Three public `/futures/data/` series of the already-authorized `binance_futures` provider — `topLongShortPositionRatio` (position bias of the top 20% of accounts by margin balance), `topLongShortAccountRatio` (the same cohort counted by account), `globalLongShortAccountRatio` (every account counted one each). **The pair is the point, not any single series:** large capital and account headcount disagreeing is a statement no column in the feature row can currently make, because every one of them would read agreement and disagreement identically. `takerlongshortRatio` is deliberately **not** collected — it restates the aggressor split the kline legs already carry, and those come with 500 days for free while this family keeps 30, so collecting it would be paying a retention wall for a worse copy of something already held. **The whole shape: it feeds nothing.** No feature reads it, no family mints against it, `snapshot` is never touched, and a test asserts that last part rather than a comment claiming it — because the day someone wires it is the day that test should turn red and make them read the docstring first. The reason is arithmetic, not caution: the endpoints keep 30 days and the factory replays 500, so a wired feature would be indeterminate over 94% of every replay window and the robustness scorer would be judging the retention wall instead of the strategy. The reason to start today rather than when a family wants the data is that **retroactive collection is impossible** — 500 days of depth is 500 days away whenever it starts, so waiting costs precisely the thing that is scarce. Progress is a field on the daily board (`포지셔닝 N/500일`), deliberately not a warning, for the reason the hourly-OI line beside it carries: a shortfall that will be true every morning for over a year is how a board teaches its reader to skip the warning block. **Two things this got wrong on the first pass and the repo caught.** (1) `attach_feeds` accumulated unconditionally, so ten existing test call sites started writing the repo's real `.runtime_governance_state/` — caught by conftest's `_tests_never_touch_the_real_state_dir` guard, which is exactly what it is for. The fix is not ten patched tests: everything else `attach_feeds` does only mutates `snapshot`, so accumulation is now **opt-in** (`accumulate=False`) and the live cycle is the single caller that turns it on. That is the `routing_marks` rule from `paper.run_paper_update` — a dry run keeps no marks because it keeps no state — and the default's failure direction is quiet rather than dangerous, since a production caller that forgot it shows as flat coverage on the board. The factory fire deliberately does **not** accumulate: one accumulator on the 15-minute cadence is what the hourly throttle is sized for, and a second would only exercise it. (2) The **trailing incomplete period must be dropped**, and this is the one bug that would have silently poisoned a 500-day accumulation from its first hour: the store dedupes on `(symbol, series, timestamp)` and is append-only, so a partial reading written now would be the value kept *forever* while the venue's later corrected reading for that same timestamp is discarded as a duplicate. Dropping it is what makes the accumulation converge on the venue's own numbers rather than on whatever it happened to see first. Everything else is `oi_store`'s shape, inherited rather than reinvented because the problem is the same one: append-only JSONL with latest-wins reads (so a re-fetch is idempotent and a gap shorter than retention self-heals), `record_sha256` per row, reads that skip damage instead of raising (the only consumer is a coverage number, and under-reporting is the safe direction), and `covered_days` measured as the oldest→newest **span** with `gap_periods` beside it, never a row count ÷ 24 that a hole in the middle would flatter. Two deliberate deltas: each series degrades **independently** and the call's overall status is the worst of the three, so one endpoint refusing neither costs the other two their readings nor reports as success; and `longShortRatio` is dropped on the way in even though the venue sends it, because it is exactly `longAccount / shortAccount` and a third stored field can disagree with the other two after rounding. **The throttle measures from the last ATTEMPT, and this branch had it wrong until `main` moved.** The first version keyed on the newest stored *reading's* age, justified in these notes as keeping one throttle shape with `oi_store` — and then #355 landed, having found that exact design never skips: the venue's newest complete period is always at least one period behind the clock (the forming one is dropped), so "the newest row is over an hour old" is true for almost every minute of every hour and all four contexts of a symbol re-ask inside one fire. The precedent this store cited changed under it, so the store followed rather than keeping a design whose own author had just fixed it: the marker is a small JSON map keyed per **(symbol, series)**, stamped **before** the request so a fetch that hangs or raises still counts, and written on failure too — a success-only marker turns a refusing endpoint into 240 asks an hour across three series and twenty contexts. Damaged marks read as *ask again*, which is fail-OPEN and the opposite of the direction this store's row reads take: a corrupt marker reading as "recently attempted" would silently stop a 500-day accumulation while the board went on reporting the coverage it already had. And whether a call seeds is a property of the **store**, not of the marks, so a machine that lost its marker file refreshes rather than re-requesting thirty days it already holds.
- Also done: **Derivative pricing and cross-asset context — the second half of the data widening** (`crypto/market_data.py`, `crypto/features.py`, `crypto/indicators.py`, `crypto/cycle.py`, `crypto/factory.py`, `scheduler.py`; 2026-07-30). Tier 1 of `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md`, after the taker-flow entry below. Two groups, one theme: **both replaced a fabricated constant with a measurement**, which is the reason each was worth a request rather than merely nice to have. **(1) Mark / index / premium index** (`markPriceKlines`, `indexPriceKlines`, `premiumIndexKlines` — kline-shaped public endpoints of the already-authorized `binance_futures` provider, so no new provider, key or grant). `mark_price`/`index_price`/`mark_index_basis_bps` had carried the source system's documented no-feed fallbacks since C3 — `close`, `close`, and a hardcoded `0.0` — **while `factory.NUMERIC_FEATURES` admitted all three as mintable.** A literal condition on a basis that is always exactly zero does not select on anything: `basis > 0.5` could never match and `basis <= 0.5` could never fail, so the column was worse than absent, because an absent column is indeterminate and this one was confidently wrong. The premium index is the other half: it is the same crowding pressure `funding_rate` carries, at **bar** resolution instead of one 8h event, and `_asof_align` carries a funding event forward — so `funding_zscore` steps three times a day and a 1h `funding_fade_*` spec has been timing its entry on a value that last moved up to eight hours ago. `premium_fade_long`/`premium_fade_short` encode that premise on the bar being traded; **both families are kept rather than one replacing the other**, because which resolution actually pays is a question for evidence, not assertion. **(2) Cross-asset reference** (`ref_roc_4`, `rel_strength_roc_4`, `ref_correlation`, `ref_market_regime`, plus `rel_strength_long/short`). Every other column in the row describes one symbol in isolation, which is precisely why every pool strategy carries the same market beta and no feature can say so — when BTC drops, every open long is losing at once. `rel_strength_roc_4` subtracts the shared move out; `ref_correlation` measures how much there was to subtract, and is the column a future exposure rule can actually read. **Absence is indeterminate everywhere, and for the derivative group that is a deliberate behaviour change**: the old constants are gone, so a pool spec reading the basis stops trading while the series is unavailable rather than matching on a zero. Strictly safer, and "unavailable" now means the venue failed rather than the feed being unconfigured, because the series ride the candles' own grant. **Three alignment rules now live in `features`, and picking wrongly is silent** — so each is named where it applies and each has its own test: an **exact open-time join** for series on this frame's grid (`_same_grid_column`: derivative prices, reference symbol), **backward as-of** for series on their own cadence (`_asof_align`: funding, liquidations, OI), and **close-time keying** for the coarser HTF grid. The exact join is the load-bearing new one: these series are requested AT the candle interval, so their bar opening at T *is* this bar and its close is first known at the same instant — simultaneous, not look-ahead — and a bar the venue skipped stays None rather than inheriting the previous mark, which would fabricate a basis for a bar that has none. **Depth is derived, not configured:** `attach_feeds` requests `len(candles)` derivative bars, so the factory's 12,000-bar replay and the live cycle's short window stay on one code path and a `premium_*` family cannot be scored against a depth the router will not reproduce. `attach_reference` mirrors `attach_htf` (degrade-only, never raises, key absent on failure) and is a **no-op for the proxy itself**. That last point is the third instance of one rule: `rel_strength_roc_4` would be exactly `0.0` and `ref_correlation` exactly `1.0` for the benchmark against itself, so **every `ref_*` column is None there** and `REFERENCE_FAMILIES` are not minted for it — the htf_*-at-1d and session_*-at-1d gate, applied by SYMBOL, which is why `templates_for_timeframe` now takes one. `symbol=None` keeps them, because a caller that does not say what it is mining is asking for the library rather than a mintable set. `indicators.rolling_correlation` is **not a port** and says so: no source counterpart exists (the source never read a second symbol), so it follows the module's conventions — pair-counted windows, None where undefined, zero variance → None rather than a 0.0 that would claim unmeasured independence — but is excluded from the pandas-parity fixture by construction. One test moved from asserting the old semantics to asserting the new: `test_no_feed_fallbacks_match_source_absent_feed_semantics` kept only the funding and liquidation constants, and the mark/index/basis half became `test_absent_derivative_prices_are_indeterminate_not_the_old_constants` with the reasoning recorded in it. The library is now 30 families (28 for the proxy, 24 at 1d), and the rotation-coverage test mines a **non-reference** symbol so it measures the rotation rather than the gate. **No claim of edge, same as the taker entry:** on mock candles the new families land FRAGILE/PROVISIONAL on thin samples, which is the right verdict for a hash-derived random walk. **One thing this increment had to bring with it, discovered by merging `main`:** #354 had just landed `PerSymbolFeedCache` because "forty vendor requests to read ten symbols" — and the reference leg reproduces that redundancy by a different door. The proxy symbol is a *constant*, so across a fan-out the only distinct reads are one per timeframe, while `attach_reference` runs once per (symbol, timeframe): sixteen asks for four answers on a 5×4 grid. `ReferenceCandleCache` is the same contract as the feed cache and deliberately not a generalization of it — one fan-out then discarded (so it cannot serve a stale bar to a later fire), failures cached too (a venue that just refused will refuse again in the same second, and re-asking once per remaining context is how a cap gets reached), refusal re-raised unchanged so every context still records its own reason code. The derivative price series need no such cache and that is not an oversight: they are requested AT the candle interval, so the four timeframes are four genuinely different series rather than one asked four times. Left alone, and noted here because the next reader will wonder: **`funding_history` still has the uncached redundancy** — it takes a symbol and a record count and no timeframe, so the fan-out asks each symbol's 8h series once per timeframe. That predates this work and fixing it is a separate change.
- Also done: **Taker order flow and session context — the first feature groups that are not price** (`crypto/market_data.py`, `crypto/features.py`, `crypto/factory.py`; 2026-07-30). Found by asking what would raise expected return rather than what was broken, and the answer was embarrassing: **the collector was downloading the data and throwing it away.** A Binance USD-M kline row is twelve fields and `_parse` read seven — `[7] quote_volume`, `[8] trade_count`, `[9] taker_buy_base`, `[10] taker_buy_quote` were parsed off the end of a comment that said `...`. `[9]` over `[5]` is the bar's order-flow imbalance, and it is the only thing here **price history cannot reconstruct**: two bars with identical OHLC are completely different events depending on which side crossed the spread. **Why that is the shape of the gap and not just a missing feature:** roughly 25 of the row's 33 columns are transformations of one series, so the twenty template families are recombinations of information the pool already held — which is why adding a twenty-first indicator has never been the way to raise expectancy and why this is. Cost of the fix: zero extra requests, zero new vendor, zero new grant, and 500 days of history available on the next factory run. **Only the normalized derivatives are mintable** (`taker_buy_ratio`, `taker_flow_imbalance`, `taker_flow_zscore`, `taker_flow_ma`, `avg_trade_size_zscore`, `trade_count_zscore`); raw `quote_volume`/`trade_count`/`taker_buy_base`/`taker_buy_quote`/`avg_trade_size` ride the row as evidence and stay out of `NUMERIC_FEATURES` — the raw-`open_interest` rule, for its exact reason, that a threshold on a venue-scale quantity means one thing on BTC, another on SOL and another again after the venue grows. **Absence is indeterminate, never a constant**: a pre-flow snapshot or a venue that stops sending the legs leaves every derived column None, so a flow spec stops trading. That is the open-interest posture and deliberately **not** the `liquidation_spike_ratio` one, which fills with 0.0 and lets a mined condition match on a fabricated value — the hazard this repo already carries once and must not carry twice. Buy volume outside `[0, volume]` reads as unknown rather than being clamped: clamping a broken payload to 1.0 manufactures a maximally bullish reading, which is the direction that trades. The parser stays **strict on OHLCV and lenient on the flow legs** — a row missing the price legs is still malformed, but a payload change must not stop the twenty families that never look at flow. **Session is categorical on purpose.** A numeric `hour_of_day` would admit `== 3`, a free pick of one bucket in twenty-four that `count_free_parameters` charges as a single literal — the cheapest possible way to mine noise. Three labels with only `==`/`!=` bound it to a choice among three, and **which** session a spec claims runs through a mutated `session_index` parameter rather than a hand-written `"US"`, so the choice is a literal on the emitted condition and the scorer sees that a decision was made (asserted: `count_free_parameters` returns 5 for a `session_trend_*` spec). `session` goes None above `MAX_SESSION_BAR_MINUTES` and the `session_*` families are dropped from the 1d rotation — the htf_* rule, because at 1d every bar opens at 00:00 UTC and the label would be the constant "ASIA", i.e. the spike-ratio hazard arriving by a different door. **Two consequences worth knowing before something looks wrong.** The candle dicts changed, so `integrity.sha256_record({"candles": ...})` changed, so `evidence_input_sha256` and the factory seed differ from every prior run: candidates minted before and after are no longer grouped as near-duplicates by `pool.behavioural_fingerprint`. That is correct (they were scored on different inputs) but it will look like duplicate detection reset. And the library is now **26 families, not a multiple of the batch size of 4**, which turned `test_every_family_in_the_library_is_reachable_across_generations` red on `len(minted) == len(set(minted))` — seven batches mint 28 specs over 26 families, so two repeats are arithmetic, not a rotation defect. The assertion was restated as what the rotation actually owes: the first `len(templates)` mints are distinct, i.e. nothing repeats until the library has been covered. Verified red against the pre-fix rotation. **No claim of edge is made here.** On mock candles the new families score FRAGILE with thin samples, which is the correct verdict for a hash-derived random walk and is evidence the anti-overfit machinery works, not that these families do. Whether the flow columns carry signal is a question for real replay evidence — and per `docs/TRADING_ALPHA_RESEARCH_RECORD.md` each is minted as its own family first, never grafted onto a proven lineage, so a new information source has to earn a verdict on its own before fusion may carry it anywhere. Also fixed in passing: `proposer.py`'s docstring told readers the three liquidation columns were outside the validator's vocabulary; they were admitted 2026-07-24 and the note was never updated, so it had been misinforming proposal review for six days.
- Also done: **The live leg enforces the strategy's time exit** (`crypto/live_position.py`, `crypto/live_entry.py`, `crypto/live_leg.py`, `crypto/live_route.py`, `crypto/paper.py`; 2026-07-29, Thomas chose the market-close form). Paper has always enforced `max_holding_bars`; live did not, and `live_route`'s docstring said so plainly — correctly, for the routing increment, because a live position record carried no holding count and no timeframe, so a max-hold rule would have been inventing state rather than reading it. **What made the absence a gap rather than a difference: the promotion evidence gating live trading was produced with the time exit in force**, so a live leg without one was not running the strategy the evidence describes — and unfavourably, since a time exit mostly cuts losers, so live held them longer than any backtest did. LP5.1's record now carries `timeframe`, `max_holding_bars`, `holding_candles` and the dedup key; `live_route` advances the counter each cycle and closes reduceOnly at market when it reaches the limit. **`max_holding_bars` rides on the position, not re-read at exit time** — paper's parity rule (`position_max_hold`), so a spec edited mid-hold cannot move the exit of a trade already open; it travels plan → decision (`exit_terms`) → position, read once like every other fact the leg shares. Two properties were carried over deliberately because the counter *is* the rule, and each has its own test: the count **advances on cycles that close nothing** and is persisted unconditionally, so a venue that cannot be reached does not reset the clock; and **one bar counts once** — `paper._advance_holding` was promoted to a shared `paper.advance_holding` taking the timestamp rather than the candle, because paper dedups on a candle's `close_time` and the live leg on the feature row's `timestamp`, which is that same value under another key. One function, so the two legs cannot drift on what "a bar passed" means. Ordering inside `_settle_or_protect` is deliberate: venue-closed first, then unprotected (the more urgent close, and an incident if it fails), then the time exit — which also fires for a position whose protection could not be *read*, since the bracket is a price rule and max-hold is a time rule and neither substitutes for the other. **The one survivable failed close in this module**: a time exit that will not confirm is reported (`LIVE_ROUTING_TIME_EXIT_DEFERRED`) and retried next cycle rather than halting the fan-out — unlike an unprotected position it still has its bracket resting, so it is protected, merely held past its window. A legacy position falls back to the timeframe table and names the fallback (`LIVE_ROUTING_MAX_HOLD_FALLBACK`) so a live/backtest R gap stays attributable. Still not identical to paper and recorded as such: paper models the exit at the bar's close, live pays taker plus slippage — same rule, different cost, `r_basis` keeps the populations labelled.
- Also done: **The `live_trading` grant was removed; the env opt-in is the whole gate** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/safety_gate.py`, the five `crypto/live_*.py` selectors, `governance/GOVERNANCE_POLICY.yaml` → `1.2.0`, `tests/conftest.py`; **Thomas decision 2026-07-28, stated four times and confirmed in full scope**). Live trading was gated by an env opt-in **and** a per-machine integrity-checked grant record, and the grant is now gone: `MVP_LIVE_TRADING=real` alone selects the real order adapter, P&L ledger, position book, daily counter and canary registry. **This is a relaxation of a safety control and is recorded as one** — not as a refactor, and not softened. Two reasons, the second one decisive: the grant is TTL-capped at 30 days on a system meant to run unattended for months; and a grant expiring while a position was **open** shut the CLOSE path too, because `evaluate_live_close_guard` exempts a reduceOnly close from the loss breaker, the daily count, the exposure cap, the promotion gate and both kill switches — every halt the design deliberately made non-trapping — and then still required the gate. Expiry walked around all of it and could strand a real position at 00:00 with nothing having gone wrong. **What was given up, so a future reader restoring the grant knows what they are restoring:** a second factor, an expiry, an audited per-machine record of scope and authority level, and mid-flight revocation — `assert_authorization` re-reads the env instead of the record now, and a running process's environment does not change under it, so clearing the variable stops the *next* process, not this one. **What was not given up:** revocation itself. The console `kill` verb is file-based, lands on a running scheduler at its next guard, and is exempt on the close path — the one thing grant expiry could never do. It is now the halt of record, and the readiness board prints it in place of "delete the grant file", with an explicit **do not clear `MVP_LIVE_TRADING` to halt** (it needs a restart *and* it shuts the close path, stranding open positions). **Blast radius was the main design constraint.** `select_env_gated` is a separate function from `select_gated`, deliberately not a `require_grant=False` keyword on it: a keyword would put "no grant needed" one token away from the model provider, the search tool, the operator channel and the workspace writer, where a diff would not show it. `test_only_live_trading_uses_the_env_only_gate` walks the AST of every module under `runtime/` and asserts the caller set is exactly the five live modules. The provider id and the `network_access`+`filesystem_write` pair survive the removal — still re-checked at egress, still declared by each capable class — so the capability still cannot be half-enabled; only what *opens* the gate changed. All five selectors moved together on purpose: an adapter that trades while the ledger stays inert is real money with the loss breaker reading zero, an inert position book is positions the runtime cannot see to close, and an inert canary registry is orders placed and not counted as the evidence they are. `binance_futures_account` **kept** its grant, so the board legitimately checks a grant on one row and an env var on another — that asymmetry is the decision, not drift. Two consequences were caught while writing it and fixed here rather than later: `MVP_LIVE_TRADING` was added to the autouse gate-env clear in `tests/conftest.py`, because while the grant existed a suite run on a machine with the variable exported still got inert objects and now would not — and the operator's own machine is exactly where both this variable and this suite get run; and `tests/test_deployment_env_passthrough.py`'s claim that "env alone opens nothing — which is why forwarding is a cost rather than a capability" is no longer true, since forwarding the `.env` to the scheduler is now by itself the difference between paper and real money. Governance: `p5_policy_gate.requires` drops `operator_safety_flag_grant` for `live_trading_opt_in` (policy `1.1.0` → `1.2.0`, removed requirement's rationale preserved in comments); `runtime_effect` stays `REVIEW_ONLY` and every grant flag stays false.
- Also done: **The halt peek — a `/kill` reaches the runtime before the analysis it interrupted ends** (`operator.peek_for_halt` + `PEEKABLE_HALT_VERBS`, `OperatorChannel.peek` / `TelegramChannel.peek` / `MockOperatorChannel.peek`, wired into `run_queued_task`'s stage callback; **Thomas decision K2′, 2026-07-29**, option E of `docs/proposals/CONTROL_LANE_SEPARATION_V0.1.md`). The gap was **not** "a long analysis cannot be interrupted" — it was that while one runs, `/kill` existed **nowhere in the runtime**. The operator loop is the only process that receives Telegram and it does not poll while it drains, so the control state file could not change for the length of an analysis. That file is what the crypto cycle's `live_route` re-reads immediately before a live entry, in the *other* container — which made this loop's responsiveness a dependency of the money path. The fix rides the stage boundaries the progress line already installed: at each one, read what is waiting and, if a halt is there, write the state. **The cursor is the entire risk and the entire design.** `peek` reads at the current offset with `timeout=0` and advances **nothing**, in memory or on disk: advancing would claim the whole batch while handling only the halt verb in it, silently destroying an ordinary request sent in the same second — the outcome the F1 enqueue rule exists to prevent. Because nothing is claimed, everything the peek sees arrives again at the next poll, so the peek may act only on what is safe to act on **twice**: it writes state through the same `control.apply_command` (with `ledger=None`) and never a ledger event or a reply, both of which belong to the normal handling that follows, once. `PEEKABLE_HALT_VERBS` is `{kill, pause}` and the exclusions are the safety argument, not an oversight — **`resume` is absent because a peek that could resume would let a halt be undone by a message sent *before* it, re-read out of order**; `approve` because consuming an approval twice is what `one_time_use_required` forbids; `status`/`audit` because they reply. The identity gate runs inside the peek, so an unverified `/kill` is not a shortcut into the control state. Every exception is swallowed — a safety net that can destroy the run it protects is not one — and a transport with no `peek` degrades to exactly the previous behaviour. **What this deliberately does not do: stop the running analysis.** That needs an abort path and a registry terminal for an interrupted RUNNING entry, which is decision K4 and still open; the drain already re-reads the control state before claiming the next task, so nothing further starts. Both load-bearing properties are mutation-checked: making `peek` advance the cursor, and adding `resume` to the verb set, each turn tests red. The cursor test runs against the **real** `TelegramChannel` with `_call` intercepted, because the mock cannot fail the way the real transport would.
- Also done: **The front-desk prompt never named what the parser requires** (`frontdesk._PROMPT_TEMPLATE` + `_prompt_header` interpolating `providers._REQUIRED_ANALYSIS_KEYS`; 2026-07-29). Found by driving the **live** provider rather than the scripted one every test uses, which is the only way this class of defect is visible at all. Measured, paced, with reason codes, 8 turns per variant: the deployed prompt produced a valid turn **3 of 8**. Two distinct failures, both invisible to the suite. **(1) `MALFORMED_RESPONSE`, 3 of 8 — the dominant one.** The turn rides inside the shared analysis JSON, so the provider's parser requires that JSON's own fields (`summary`/`key_findings`/`facts`), and this prompt described only `recommendation` and never mentioned them; Groq's `json_object` mode constrains no keys, so the model was free to omit them and did. The whole response is then discarded, the turn never reaches the runtime, and the channel degrades to the plain queue — safe, and silent. Naming the fields took it to **0 of 8**. **(2) The two schema-required flags omitted, 2 of 8.** The prompt said only *when* to set `important`/`independent_validation` true, so a small model read "neither applies" as "leave them out" and the turn failed validation. Saying they are mandatory and that false is a value took that to **0 of 8**. Combined: **8 of 8**. Both lists are **interpolated from the authority rather than typed out** — the envelope keys from the parser's own constant, joining the schema version and the request kinds, each of which is there because the same drift already happened once. Honest limits, recorded because the number will be quoted: n=8 per variant, and the 8/8 was measured on text equivalent in content to what shipped but not byte-identical — the confirming run on the shipped text was **blocked by rate limiting the measurement itself caused**, which is the other lesson here. Load-testing a prompt against the *live operator's* provider key exhausted the free-tier quota and left the production front desk degraded (fail-closed, no message lost) until it reset. A separate key, or a separate provider, is what that work needed.
- Also done: **Canary evidence, per record — "did the one I just placed record its fill?"** (`live_promotion.canary_evidence_rows` / `render_canary_evidence_text` / `main`, `python -m runtime.mvp_runtime.crypto.live_promotion`; 2026-07-29, during a live canary run). The readiness board already reports the **aggregate** — `4 of 4 cannot prove their size` — which answers *is the evidence sound* but not *did the canary I just placed record its fill*. During a live run that second question is the whole question, and answering it meant opening the jsonl by hand. **The four records standing as evidence on this machine are `0/4` provable**, and the cause is timing rather than a defect: `build_canary_order_record` gained `quantity`/`fill` in #268 (merged 2026-07-27 08:28:36Z) and all four were recorded before the process running them carried that code — including one written 31 minutes *after* the merge, which is the merge-is-not-deploy distinction showing up in the money path's own evidence. Verified that `scripts/place_canary_order.py` **does** pass `quantity=` and `fill=` today, so the remaining hypothesis (a still-broken path that would keep producing unprovable evidence) is **ruled out** — a canary placed now records the venue's numbers. `size_proven` is derived from the record, never asserted: true only when the venue's filled notional is present, because only then is declared-versus-filled a subtraction. An old record renders as `no fill recorded` rather than as a blank, which would read as a zero-sized order; an `unclean` record is shown and marked rather than hidden, because hiding it would make the board disagree with the registry it renders. Read-only, ungated, no socket — asserted by a test on the source. That test is itself a small lesson: its first version forbade the token `append` and matched `rows.append(...)`, a list method, failing on its own subject — the same shape as the order-path tripwire tripping on a docstring, and a check that fails on what it is checking teaches the next author to delete it. The repository's own gate then taught the convention this missed: every entry point must call `force_utf8_io()` (a board with an em dash is not cp949-encodable), asserted for all of them rather than the ones that print non-ASCII today.
- Also done: **The evidence question moved from canaries to real trades** (`live_promotion.live_trade_evidence_rows` / `render_live_trade_evidence_text`, folded into the same `python -m runtime.mvp_runtime.crypto.live_promotion` board; `_money` lifted to module level; 2026-07-29, **Thomas decision: no further canaries will be placed**). The four canary records are frozen at **0/4 provable** and cannot be repaired — the venue's figures were never stored — so with no more canaries coming, "can the live path prove what it did" has to be answered by **real trades**. The board now has both halves, and the second one is the one that matters from here. Real trades answer it **by construction, more strongly than the canary path ever did**: `build_live_position` takes `entry_price`/`quantity` from the actual fill and never from the intent, and `live_leg` refuses to confirm an entry unless the venue reported a positive filled quantity *and* price — a position that cannot show a fill is not booked at all. So an UNPROVEN row here does **not** mean the size drifted; it means something wrote an outcome by a path that skipped that rule, and the rendering says so rather than leaving it to be read as a rounding complaint. Zero live trades have closed on this machine, so the **empty state is the state during live testing** and is written to be informative rather than blank. Reads outcomes only (`live_pnl.read_live_outcomes`) — the cycle already reads live outcomes so the risk guard can see live losses, and reading a result is not reaching the order path, which is the same boundary the tripwire draws by leaving `live_pnl` out of its module set. Two corrections while building it, both the same shape as ones this repository has already recorded: `_money` was **nested inside** `build_canary_order_record`, so the readers could not share the rule that decides what counts as a number — a board rendering `"66.83"` as a number where the record refuses to store one would report agreement the record never claimed; and the boundary test's first version searched the source **text**, failing on the function's own docstring for naming the module that enforces the fill rule — rewritten to walk the **import graph**, exactly as `main` had already rewritten the order-path tripwire after it tripped on a docstring.
- Done: R0.5 (repo sync/cleanup), R1 (MVP spec), **R2 full single-agent pipeline** (R2.1 Intake → R2.7 E2E: intake, Thomas Prime planner, role routing, model invocation behind the Safety-Flag Gate, output validation, hash-chained audit, durable append-only ledger), **R3 Read-only web-search tool** (mock path complete — search is an `INTERNAL_READ` ALLOW action whose hits become evidence and whose use is audited; real backends are gated per provider — `tavily_search` (recommended free backend since 2026-07-21, after Brave dropped its free tier) or `brave_search` — activate locally with `scripts/activate_safety_flag.py`; a backend failure at run time **degrades** the run to no live evidence with `SEARCH_DEGRADED` audited, never blocks it), **R4 Operator/Telegram control channel** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/operator.py` + `operator_cli.py`: canonical identity gate — Telegram private 1:1, registered user+chat, unverified silently dropped — the real Telegram adapter behind the Safety-Flag Gate, and the poll→handle→send loop entrypoint; see `docs/runtime-contracts/OPERATOR_CONTROL_CHANNEL_V0.1.md`), and **R5 Memory** (R5.1–R5.4: working-memory candidate creation (ALLOW), durable per-machine store + scoped retrieval feeding back as context, operator-only promotion of candidates to VALIDATED, each memory event audited; `runtime/mvp_runtime/memory.py` + `working_memory.py` + `scripts/promote_memory_candidate.py`; **R5 retention** (§12.4): candidates carry `expires_at`, retrieval never serves an expired candidate, and `runtime/mvp_runtime/memory_cli.py prune` deletes expired candidates with an audited memory-retention event — VALIDATED memory untouched; **R5 validated read-back**: `retrieve_validated_memory` closes the promotion loop — operator-promoted VALIDATED memory (scope `related_validated_memory`, already granted by every role's `memory_policy.readable_scopes`) feeds later runs as prompt context ([V#] block, framed reliable vs. the unverified [M#] candidates) + `validated_memory` evidence; same scope-gate/recency-cap/fail-closed semantics as candidate retrieval, no expiry (retention never touches VALIDATED)). Architecture-review remediations A (enforced Safety-Flag Gate), B (durable ledger + audit-every-outcome), and D (single authority for levels/invariant/effect in `authority.py`) also merged; C parked — mass deletion is low-ROI and the dormant material is gate-pinned (evidence: `docs/ARCHITECTURE_REVIEW_RECORD.md`).
- Also done: **R4 emergency operator console** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/control.py` + `console_cli.py`): pause/kill/resume/status/stop over the verified Telegram channel or a local host CLI (SSH emergency stop), enforced by the loop off a local gitignored control state (`.runtime_governance_state/operator_control_state.json`) with fail-closed semantics (missing=ACTIVE, corrupt=KILLED) and durable tamper-evident control events. Honors `kill_switch.agent_can_disable_or_bypass: false` — only the authenticated operator can resume.
- Remaining R4: **none** — both items once deferred here have shipped: control-channel approval handling landed with R9 (`/approve`/`/reject` over the verified channel) and the `audit`/`recovery` console verbs landed as their own increment (see the bullets below).
- Also done: **R4.5 Server Deploy** — `Dockerfile` (python:3.12-slim, non-root, runs the continuous operator loop) + `requirements-runtime.txt` (pinned prod deps: YAML + JSON Schema only) + `.dockerignore` + `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`. The image carries only committed non-secret source; secrets (bot token, API key) come from env and per-machine state (Core pointer, registration, safety-flag activation, control state, ledger) is a mounted volume — never baked in, so a bare image fails closed on network capabilities. Emergency stop on a running service via `docker exec ... console_cli kill`.
- Also done: **R6 Scheduler** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/scheduler.py` + `scheduler_cli.py`): recurring schedules (interval cadence) run a stored task **template** — `analysis_task` (through the full pipeline) or `memory_prune` (the periodic driver for R5 retention), never a shell command. **Kill-switch bound** (`kill_blocks: scheduler_execution` — PAUSED/KILLED skips a due fire and drops the occurrence), overlap-safe (single-process sequential), every fire/skip audited; schedules in `.runtime_governance_state/schedules.jsonl` (local, gitignored). Thin live scheduler — the deferred `scheduler_plan_review.v0.1` review-only schema stays inactive. See `docs/runtime-contracts/SCHEDULER_V0.1.md`.
- Also done: **R7 Multi-Agent — independent validation agent** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/validator.py` + planning in `prime.py`/`permission.py`): the minimal two-agent team the governance defines — opt-in (`--independent-validation` on the CLI / operator loop), the active `validation.independent` role reviews the specialist's output in a fresh context (own P2 `SIMULATION_VALIDATION` PermissionDecision, own assignment/actor/budget), verdict merged **stricter-wins** with the automatic checks, independence verified programmatically, both validations + the validator's model call audited. Zero new contracts/roles/gates; provider chokepoints untouched (verdict rides in the shared analysis JSON). Deliberately excluded: revision cycles, parallel agents, agent fleets, inter-agent messaging. **R7.1/R7.2 selective validation**: `--independent-validation auto` reviews only when the task warrants it — the classification decides first (ORANGE/RED risk per policy §3.4, or operator-marked importance: priority HIGH/URGENT via a leading `!중요`/`!important` token on the control channel or `--important` on the CLI); otherwise the **orchestrator triage** (R7.2) judges: Prime plans a governed triage action (own `INTERNAL_ANALYSIS` P2 ALLOW PermissionDecision; permdec id seed now includes `action_type` so same-scope actions can't collide) and one small budgeted model call (`TRIAGE_TOKEN_ALLOWANCE`) on the validator/triage provider returns HIGH (reviewer runs) or NORMAL (skipped). Under "auto" the team is always *planned*, the triage decides what *runs*; allocation is a ceiling, spend is in `budget_usage`; triage failure degrades to NORMAL with `TRIAGE_DEGRADED` audited — never blocks, never silently doubles spend. `MVP_VALIDATOR_PROVIDER` gives the validator (and triage) its own gated provider (own grants, chain semantics identical to `MVP_HOSTED_PROVIDER`), spreading review load onto a different quota/model family. See `docs/runtime-contracts/INDEPENDENT_VALIDATION_V0.1.md`.
- Also done: **R8 Controlled Write** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/workspace.py`): the runtime's first **EXECUTE_AND_REPORT** action (everything before it is ALLOW-tier) and its first durable artifact outside its own state. `--write-output PATH` creates the rendered response under the gitignored repo-root `workspace/`. Confined to `workspace/` (absolute/`..`/symlink-escape all rejected post-resolution), kill-switch bound, gated OFF by default behind a new `filesystem_write` safety flag (`DryRunWriter` is the default; the env var alone fails closed), written only when validation PASSES, and audited + reported. See `docs/runtime-contracts/CONTROLLED_WRITE_V0.1.md`.

The point is safety, not DRY: `gated_factory` *receives* the `Authorization`, so "never construct the capable implementation before the gate opens" is structural rather than four authors remembering the right order. A fifth capability gets it right by construction.
- Also done: **R4 `audit`/`recovery` console verbs** (`control.py` `audit_lines`/`recovery_lines` + `audit.verify_audit_chain` + `store.read_audit_events`/`health`): the last two verbs `emergency_controls_allowed` names. Both **read-only** and both answer while PAUSED/KILLED (`kill_allows: [read_only_status, audit_read]`); neither writes a ledger event (the `status` precedent — a read that appends to the log it reads races its own chain tip). `audit` **verifies the whole chain** — the runtime had built a hash chain since R2.6 and never once checked it. Four checks: `AUDIT_EVENT_HASH_MISMATCH`, `AUDIT_PAYLOAD_RECORD_MISMATCH`, `AUDIT_APPEND_ONLY_BOUNDARY_MISMATCH`, `AUDIT_PREVIOUS_HASH_MISMATCH` (codes reused from `protected_governance_state/recovery.py`). **The payload↔record check is load-bearing:** a self-hash check alone misses an edited *visible* record entirely, since the untouched payload still hashes fine — the existing deferred verifier has that blind spot. Known limit stated, not hidden: a prefix of a valid chain is valid, so a truncated tail needs a separate signal. `recovery` **diagnoses, never repairs** — repairing the ledger would be `audit_concealment`, and rollback proper is unreachable (`ROLLBACK_RECOVERY_CONTRACT_V0.1` requires the deferred `execution_request.v0.1`, the same blocker as R9's preview; `RUNTIME_ENTRY_CRASH_RECOVERY` is `SYNTHETIC_TEST_ONLY` over SQLite the MVP doesn't use; governance says `rollback` zero times). Zero new contracts/schemas/registries/gates; Telegram picks both verbs up free via `COMMANDS`, still behind R4's identity gate. See `docs/runtime-contracts/AUDIT_RECOVERY_CONSOLE_V0.1.md`.

Deliberately the narrowest crossing, and gated: the capable consumer is built only behind a new **`approval_consumption` safety flag** (opt-in env + local integrity-checked activation; env var alone fails closed), so an APPROVED approval **still** authorizes nothing until deliberately spent. Hot-path revalidated (re-derives the action fingerprint + re-hashes the *current* candidate content — `FINGERPRINT_MISMATCH`/`CONTENT_CHANGED`/`CANDIDATE_GONE`; latest-wins candidate lookup), single-use (compare-and-set re-read → `ALREADY_CONSUMED`, and the grant is marked CONSUMED **before** the promotion is written so a partial failure leaves it spent-but-unpromoted rather than re-spendable), and **kill-switch bound** (checked first; refused mode-aware as `RUNTIME_KILLED`/`RUNTIME_PAUSED` — the one refusal vocabulary at every door — like R8 `tool_write` and R6 `scheduler_execution`). One additive schema bump (`approval.v0.2`: `CONSUMED` state + `consumed_at`/`consumption_ref`); `permission_decision.v0.3` needed no bump (consumption is an approval-record transition; the decision is immutable planning evidence). Both gates updated to expect the implemented flag + understand `CONSUMED` while still pinning every runtime-effect grant false. Editing the governance policy changed its SHA-256, so the read-only replay bundle was regenerated (`rebuild_bundle`). See `docs/runtime-contracts/APPROVAL_CONSUMPTION_V0.1.md`.

Each COMPLETED+PASS run (opt-in `ProgramizationStore`, wired by CLI/operator/scheduler; local gitignored state) records one observation and folds it into its pattern counter under a cross-process lock; **ten valid independent repetitions flip the pattern to TRIGGERED exactly once** and put the audit event (`REVIEW_ONLY`/`NO_PROGRAM_CREATED`) on that run's chain — a review *opportunity*, never a Program (`PROGRAMIZATION_REVIEW_TRIGGER_ONLY`; activation stays APPROVAL_REQUIRED, unregistered execution stays BLOCK, `allowed_program_ids` stays `[]`). Counting is fail-closed toward not-counting (retry/duplicate-trace/identical-input/synthetic-mock all excluded from the store's own history; undetectable exclusions documented); records are schema-validated before persisting and ride the run's records ledger; the seam itself is best-effort (`programization_error`, the working-memory precedent — never blocks delivery). **Review handling** (increment 2, explicit Thomas decision 2026-07-22; `programization_cli.py`): forward-only operator transitions (TRIGGERED→UNDER_REVIEW→CLOSED; CLOSED terminal; counter never touches operator-owned statuses), one DRAFT `programization_candidate.v0.1` per pattern only while UNDER_REVIEW (Thomas authors the §5 substance in an input file; runtime pins `activation_eligibility: candidate_only…` + `permission_expansion: false` — a candidate grants nothing), every mutation identity+reason-required, kill-switch bound, audited as `programization_review_event.v0` on its own ledger stream. **Candidate shadow-validation path** (increment 3, explicit Thomas decision 2026-07-22): forward-only DRAFT→REVIEW_READY→VALIDATING(shadow RUNNING)→shadow PASS/FAIL→ACCEPTED/REJECTED (terminal). The runtime never *runs* a shadow (Programs are unregistered; unregistered execution is BLOCK) — it enforces the operator's limited comparison: outcome only while VALIDATING+RUNNING, non-empty `comparison_ref`+`result`, single-shot, and **`accept` requires shadow PASS** (no acceptance by assertion). ACCEPTED is a review milestone, not a capability (schema constants pin `candidate_only…`/`permission_expansion: false`). **Threshold amendment + program request** (increment 4, explicit Thomas decisions 2026-07-22): trigger threshold **10 → 5** as a version bump (`programization_pattern.v0.2` const 5 + `programization_candidate.v0.2` min 5; v0.1 rows stay valid history, runtime migrates them forward on next touch; governance key `ten_valid_repetitions_result` keeps its historical name). **Program request** (`program_request.py` + CLI `request`): from an ACCEPTED candidate, one `program_request.v0.1` — **invocation evidence, never invocation** (creation is ALLOW) — registry snapshot via the canonical resolver, lineage anchored to the pattern's last valid observation's real task, honest zero budget, and a real BLOCK PermissionDecision bound in (narrow permission widening: BLOCK buildable solely as resource-refusal evidence for `UNREGISTERED/DISABLED_RESOURCE_EXECUTION` via `build_resource_refusal_permission_decision`; insufficient authority is recorded, not raised, exactly for BLOCK evidence). Verdict is always fail-closed BLOCK while no Program is active. **Registry registration** (increment 5, explicit Thomas decision 2026-07-22; `registration.py` + `scripts/register_program_candidate.py`): the C8b verified-never-spent pattern on the registry — ask = R9 approval (scope `TOOL_PROGRAM_GOVERNANCE` joined `_APPROVAL_REQUIRED_SCOPES`, P4, no consumption impl) fingerprinting the canonical definition-content hash; apply = kill-switch first, approval **verified never spent** (content re-derived from current lineage — any drift refuses), then definition file + `candidate`/`enabled: false` registry entry written into the **working tree** (create-only, resolver self-check with rollback of the created file, ledger event; committing is Thomas's PR). Thomas authors purpose/inputs/outputs; runtime pins all load-bearing fields. Registration grants nothing (disabled-resource execution stays BLOCK). **Activation** stays the one remaining separate Thomas approval. See `docs/runtime-contracts/PROGRAMIZATION_COUNTER_V0.1.md`.
- Also done: **Core Candidate — the memory ladder's fourth rung** (`memory.build_core_candidate` / `decide_core_candidate` / `build_core_candidate_event`, `working_memory` core-candidate store + `find_core_candidate` / `live_core_candidates` / `find_validated`, `memory_cli core-list|core-propose|core-accept|core-reject`; 2026-07-27). Found by checking the design documents against the code rather than by a roadmap: architecture §8.8 and policy §12.2/§12.6 define **Session → Working → Validated → Core Candidate → Thomas Core**, three rungs existed, and the fourth appeared nowhere in `runtime/`, `scripts/` or `schemas/` — so `Validated Memory → Thomas Core` had no modelled step between "useful knowledge" and "Thomas's identity". **Zero new contracts/schemas/registries/gates**: governance already models it (`memory_learning.protected_identity_or_policy_change: APPROVAL_REQUIRED`), the memory rungs below it are schemaless records by precedent, the event rides the existing memory-event stream via `stamped_event` (the retention/learning-event precedent — a rung that changes nothing earns no heavier machinery than the rung that deletes data), and no new ledger stream or audit event type was added. Two properties carry the design. **(1) You cannot skip the ladder**: the source must be an already-promoted VALIDATED entry, so a raw model finding cannot become a proposed change to Thomas's values without someone having said yes to it once — a PROMOTED retirement marker is refused as a source too, since it shares the fields but is not the promoted knowledge. **(2) It changes nothing**: `applied_to_core: False` / `promotion_effect: "NONE"` / `permission_expansion: False` are stated as *data* on both the proposal and the decision row (ACCEPTED is the row a later reader is most likely to misread as "in the Core now"), and a test asserts no code anywhere writes `applied_to_core=True` — the absence of a writer is the real invariant, not two builders happening to agree today. Decisions are forward-only and terminal by the append-only PROMOTED-marker precedent (latest-wins lookup; a decided proposal resolves to nothing). Its own store file, so §12.4 retention cannot age out a rung that must not expire. Writing verbs are kill-switch bound with the check placed *before* the verb fan-out so the next verb inherits the gate; `core-list` answers while KILLED (`kill_allows: read_only_status`). **The runtime may never propose one**: `automatic_runtime_promotion_allowed` is false, creation requires an operator identity + rationale, and a tripwire test pins the six run-path modules against the Core-Candidate surface (the live-order-path pattern) — wiring one is a deliberate decision, not a refactor. Naming is disambiguated on purpose: a **Core Release Candidate** is a versioned Core document in the release lifecycle; a **Core Candidate** is a sentence with provenance. Promotion to Core is deliberately unbuilt — `runtime_effect.core_activation_allowed` is false and the release lifecycle owns that door.
- Also done: **Derived task risk + the §10 risk floor** (`authority.RISK_ORDER`/`stricter_risk`/`MINIMUM_RISK_FOR_DISPOSITION`/`risk_floor_for_disposition`, `permission.WORKSPACE_WRITE_RISK_LEVEL` + the floor check in `build_permission_decision`, `planner.classify_task(planned_action_risks=…)`, wired in `prime.plan_task`; 2026-07-27). **The write-up that produced this was wrong first, and the correction is the reusable part.** The gap was reported as "`classify_task` returns a constant `GREEN`, so no task can ever be high-risk" — read as a stub. Policy §10 says risk classifies **the action**, and lists "내부 분석" among its own GREEN examples, so GREEN was the *correct* answer for the specialist's action. The real defects were narrower and provable. (1) §10 also says to evaluate every perspective and take the **highest**, and a run plans more than the analysis — R3 search, R7 validation, R7.2 triage (GREEN) and the R8 controlled write. So a run that created a file was recorded as a plain read-only analysis; `classify_task` now folds in the risk of the other actions the run will plan, and `classification_reasons` names what raised it. (2) The R8 write's own decision declared `GREEN` while carrying the run path's only `EXECUTE_AND_REPORT` disposition — §10's YELLOW example is literally "내부 문서 생성". Fixed to YELLOW.
