(3) Eleven action specs each set `risk_level` by hand with nothing cross-checking them, which is how (2) shipped: there is now a **floor** — §10's risk→disposition map read backwards — enforced at the one construction site every decision passes through, so an action cannot be added below its disposition. Read **backwards** on purpose: forwards would make risk a second source of permission, which §10 explicitly forbids; backwards it grants nothing and only catches a decision contradicting itself. Behavior is deliberately unchanged: the reviewer threshold is ORANGE/RED, so YELLOW makes the record honest and mandates nothing — a test states that coupling rather than leaving it to be discovered. The derivation is **monotone by construction** (`stricter_risk` never returns below the base, and an unrecognized level resolves to ORANGE per §10's insufficient-information rule), which is what makes it safe to feed into the R7.1 reviewer decision. `complexity` and `required_capabilities` stay constant *with reasons in the docstring*, not by omission: nothing reads complexity and deriving it from free text would be a guess; deriving capabilities is §8.5's work and, with one routable role, could only refuse a run that works today.
- Also done: **§10.4 perspective separation** (`worker.PERSPECTIVES`/`PERSPECTIVE_INSTRUCTION`/`_perspectives`, `validation`'s `perspective_separation` check, `general.specialist` output contract 0.3.0 → **0.4.0**, both vendor analysis schemas; 2026-07-27). Organization Architecture §10.4 describes a complex strategy task as several perspectives reaching their own judgement before an integrated decision; the runtime had **no perspective concept at all**. Built in the form §10.4 itself permits for early MVP — *"one Agent may separate these perspectives internally"* — because §13's 3-of-6 criteria for making them three Agents are not met and §16 warns against exactly that. **The failure it prevents is specific**: a single blended narrative in which a weak revenue case is smoothed over by an enthusiastic research case, with the reader unable to see it happened. So the separation is not a prompt convention — the role contract **declares** `perspectives` (each `{perspective, verdict, basis}`, verdict POSITIVE|MIXED|NEGATIVE) and validation refuses an output that skips a perspective, or that reports a NEGATIVE perspective while stating no risk at all. Both conditions are **compliance, not analytical skill** — neither reads prose, so neither can be argued with; same nature as the existing calibration check. Scoped by `required_role_output_keys is None`, so a candidate-role trial (which passes its own declared keys) is excluded **by the role contract rather than by a hardcoded role id**. `_perspectives` **never repairs**: an unknown perspective, unknown verdict or empty basis is dropped, because "did not answer" and "answered badly" both mean the separation did not happen and a normalized entry would hide the case the check exists to catch; first entry wins per perspective so a repeat cannot overwrite its own verdict; output order is the declared order so two runs with the same verdicts produce the same record. **The load-bearing plumbing detail:** `perspectives` had to join the shared vendor response schemas, not just the specialist prompt — the OpenAI dialect is strict (`additionalProperties: false`), so a key the schema does not name is *rejected*, and the specialist would have been asked for a field the transport refused, failing its own check on every hosted run. It rides on the same terms as the other keys (required KEY, no `minItems`) so the validator and triage return `[]` rather than inventing content. `verdict` is a plain string rather than an enum: the two vendor dialects differ on enum support and an unsupported keyword fails the whole request — one REVISE beats an outage. Role version bumped with the contract (`semantic_versioning_required`), registry hash updated, and a **new test pins the contract against what the worker emits** — nothing previously compared them, which is how a drift like this starts. The change grants nothing: ceiling, allowlists, external-action and memory-write flags are asserted unchanged.
- Also done: **§8.5 routing to more than one Role** (registry + definition activation of `research.general` / `translation.general`, `planner.REQUEST_KIND_CAPABILITIES` + `capabilities_for_request_kind`, `worker.build_role_prompt`, `pipeline._role_execution` / `_provider_for_role`, `cli --kind`; **explicit Thomas decision 2026-07-27**). Two things had to be true together, and shipping either alone would have been worse than shipping neither. **Activation alone routes nothing**: every task asked for `("research","analysis")`, which only `general.specialist` has, so the newly-routable Roles were reachable by nobody. **Derivation alone breaks runs**: with one routable Role a derived capability set can only refuse work that used to succeed. Done together they compose, and the capability vocabularies turned out disjoint, so there was no `AMBIGUOUS_ROLE` risk. **A request kind names CAPABILITIES, never a Role** — the Registry stays the only thing that decides which Role covers a set, so activating or retiring a Role changes routing without touching the table; a test asserts no kind's capabilities collide with a role id, and another resolves every kind against the **live** registry so an overlapping activation fails in CI rather than at run time. The kind is **explicit** (`--kind`), not inferred from the request text: inferring "this looks like a translation" is a guess, and a wrong guess routes to a Role with a different output contract — the reader would get a confident answer of the wrong shape. An unknown kind is **refused**, never defaulted to analysis. Omitting it is byte-identical to the pre-§8.5 routing. The second half is that the selected Role runs against **its own** contract: `load_role_definition` and `role_output_spec` moved from `trial.py` to `planner.py` (they were never trial concepts, and a second copy is how "one loader for the whole runtime" stops being true), and a non-default Role gets a role-generic prompt built from its purpose, quality criteria and declared keys — distinct from the trial prompt, which additionally claims "no tools, no web access, no memory", true in an isolated trial and false for a routed run. **Found only by running it**: a routed run then failed its own output check, because the default `MockProvider` answers the business-analysis shape. The trial path already had the answer (`MockTrialProvider`, a mock built from the role's spec), so it moved to `worker.py` beside `MockProvider` and the routed path reuses it. The deeper finding is recorded rather than papered over: the **hosted** response schemas are strict (`additionalProperties: false`) and name only the analysis keys, so a hosted model structurally cannot return `translated_text` — a network provider is now refused **by name** (`ROLE_OUTPUT_CONTRACT_UNSUPPORTED_BY_PROVIDER`) instead of producing a REVISE that reads like a model quality problem when it is a transport contract problem. Making the schema role-aware is the remaining piece, and a test pins the premise so the refusal gets revisited rather than left standing once it is. Activation itself is recorded **in the registry**, not only in a commit message, including that no trial report backed it. Side effect worth knowing: `research.general` being active means a trial of it is now the contradiction `select_candidate_role` refuses, so the trial tests moved onto roles that are still candidates.
- Also done: **Role-aware hosted response schema** (`providers.analysis_response_schema` / `analysis_json_schema` / `bind_role_output_keys`, `pipeline._provider_for_role`; 2026-07-27) — the piece §8.5 shipped a named refusal for, now built. Both hosted dialects are **closed** over the analysis key set (OpenAI strict via `additionalProperties: false`, Google via the `required` list the vendor enforces), so a hosted model asked for `translated_text` returned without it and the run failed its own output check — reading like a model quality problem when it was a schema problem. The two schemas are now **derived** per call with the Role's declared keys folded in. Deliberately a derivation, not a mutation: the module constants stay the analysis shape, so the validator, the triage and every ordinary analysis run keep their body byte-for-byte, and a test asserts deriving twice does not grow the shared constant — a mutation would leak one Role's keys into every later run. Binding returns a **copy** of the provider, because the provider is selected once per process and one run's Role must not change what the next run asks for; the same `Authorization` object rides along, so binding grants nothing and the egress check still runs against the real grant. `FailoverProvider` binds **every** member and refuses the whole chain if one cannot bind: binding only the first would work until the first 503 and then quietly serve an answer shaped for a different Role — a bug that appears only during an outage — and a chain that silently shrinks is what the locked provider decision already forbids. Groq needs no fold-in (`json_object` constrains no keys; the prompt already asks, and the vendor does not reject what it does not enforce) and a test states that rather than leaving it to be rediscovered. OpenRouter's `_RESPONSE_FORMAT` constant was **deleted**, not kept beside the new override: once the schema is derived per call, a constant can only hold the unbound case and would sit there looking authoritative while nothing read it. The fail-closed direction survives: a network provider with no `bind_role_output_keys` is still refused by name.
- Also done: **§8.5 on the chat door — kind markers + the kind surviving the queue** (`operator.KIND_MARKERS` and one generalized marker loop, `task_registry_entry.v0.2`, `build_entry`/`enqueue`/`record_submission` + the drain; 2026-07-27). The CLI had `--kind`; Telegram did not, so the door Thomas actually uses could not reach the Roles activated the same day. Written up beforehand as "purely additive" — **it was not**, and the reason is the useful part: the queue is **durable and unattended**. A request queued as a translation must still be a translation when the drain picks it up minutes later, so the kind had to reach the stored entry, and `flags` could not carry it — that field is a closed set of **booleans** ("did this run have option X?"), while a kind names which capability set, and therefore which Role, the work needs. Hence `task_registry_entry.v0.2` with `request_kind` as a string beside the flags. Additive and backward-compatible: v0.1 rows already on disk carry no such key and read as `null`, which *is* the analysis routing they ran under — the migration is that there is nothing to migrate. An unroutable kind is refused at **submission**, not at drain: a bad kind accepted into a durable queue surfaces minutes later as a blocked run with nobody watching, when the operator who typed it is long gone from the channel. Blank is treated as **absent** rather than unroutable — whitespace is a caller passing nothing. On the parse side there is now **one** marker loop for both families rather than a second parser beside the first: `!중요 !번역 x` and `!번역 !중요 x` mean the same thing, at most one of each is consumed, and — the load-bearing bit — the two guards the importance marker already had (empty request, and the `!중요 /killl` hidden-command fail-open that once spent a model call on a typo'd emergency verb) now sit *inside* the loop, so they cover every marker instead of only the first. A marker must be its own leading token, so `!번역기를 만들자` stays a request about translators. The front desk still decides only for a wholly **unmarked** message: a kind marker is the operator being explicit, exactly like the importance marker, and deterministic intent never waits on a model.
- Also done: **`content.general` + `development.general` activated** (explicit Thomas decision 2026-07-27, option (b) of three offered; registry + definition flips, versions 0.3.0 → 0.4.0, hashes refreshed, two new request kinds and their operator markers). Same shape as the first activation round, and shipped **with** its routing so it is not inert. One new rule came out of it and is now enforced against the live registry: **a request kind's capability set must name at least one capability its Role does not share.** `content.general` shares `drafting` with `general.specialist`, so a content kind asking for `drafting` alone would be `AMBIGUOUS_ROLE` — the kinds use `content_planning`/`audience_adaptation` and `technical_analysis`/`implementation_planning`, all role-unique, and a test walks every kind against the resolved registry to prove exactly one Role covers it (plus a second test asserting `drafting` really is shared, so the rule is not hypothetical). **`business.analysis` was deliberately held back**, and the reason is recorded in the registry beside the other two: its capabilities overlap the MVP's core use case that `general.specialist` already serves with the §10.4 perspectives, so activating it asks "which of these two analyses does a business idea get, and why?" — a role-split question, not a routing one. Side effect worth knowing: with `content.general` active, it is the **last non-live candidate**, so the trial suite lost its second parametrized role and now rests on `business.analysis` staying a candidate; activating it means giving those tests a fixture role rather than inventing a fake registry entry now. `execution.live_trader` remains untouched and outside every routing decision.
- Also done: **The daily-loss breaker got a source, and "today" got one meaning** (`account.bucket_income` UTC-day bucket, `live_pnl.venue_daily_realized_net`, the board's gated account read, `place_canary_order`; 2026-07-27). Two findings, one root. **(1) The breaker had nothing to measure.** `live_readiness` reported `daily_loss_breaker` as NO DATA SOURCE because the local outcome ledger is *empty by construction* — its only writer is `live_leg.execute_live_exit`, the autonomous leg nothing may import, and the canary path is entry-only with positions closed by the operator on the venue. The board now reads the venue figure, **but only when an account feed is already configured**: an unconfigured machine still opens no socket and the row still fails, and a configured read that fails degrades to no figure and therefore to a *failing* row — a loss breaker is the one place where "could not measure" must never soften into "nothing to report". The read is the same gated, read-only `account` module the dashboard uses, which has no method that can place, amend or cancel anything. The board's docstring said "opens no socket"; it now says what is actually true, because that sentence is a safety claim. **(2) "Daily" meant two different things in one budget record.** `live_order.count_today` counts the daily *order* cap on `utc_day()`, while the canary path measured the daily *loss* on the account module's rolling `1d` window. Not a labelling nit: these are NET sums, so a wider window also picks up the wider window's profits and can read *less* negative — yesterday 23:00 +50 with today 01:00 −30 is **−30 on the calendar day and +20 on the rolling 24h**, today's loss hidden behind yesterday's profit on the one measure meant to stop the day (pinned by a test with those numbers). Fixed by bucketing a UTC-calendar-day window from the same income rows (no extra request) and having the breaker take the **stricter of the two** — which needs no ruling about which window is "really" a day, cannot be escaped by choosing the measurement window, and can only make the breaker trip earlier, never later. A missing or malformed bucket is skipped rather than read as zero: zero is a claim about money.
- Also done: **§8.5 in conversation — `frontdesk_turn.v0.3` carries `request_kind`** (`schemas/frontdesk_turn.v0.3.schema.json`, `frontdesk.REQUEST_KIND_GLOSSES` + `_resolve_request_kind` + the prompt/receipt, role definition 0.3.0 → 0.4.0 and its registry hash; 2026-07-27). The kind markers above put `--kind` on the chat door, but the front desk runs **only for a wholly unmarked message** — so the two halves never met: a marked request skipped the conversation, and a *conversational* submission always queued with `request_kind=None` and ran as an analysis. Of the six activated Roles, five were reachable **only** by typing a marker, i.e. not by conversation at all. "Talk to it like a chat" and "use the Roles that were built" were the same sentence and could not both be true. v0.3 adds one closed-enum field to the `SUBMIT_TASK` payload, and the layering is what keeps it a routing **signal** rather than a routing **decision**: a kind names capabilities, the Role Registry alone maps those to a Role, Prime still owns classification/permission/validation/selection, and there is still no payload field that can name a Role, tool, provider or permission (asserted directly, not just by reading the schema). **Three lists have to agree** — the prompt's, the schema's enum, and `planner.REQUEST_KIND_CAPABILITIES` — because a kind offered by one and missing from another is a submission that dies at validation or minutes later at the queue's far end, where the operator sees only a downgrade; they are built from one dict and pinned by a test. The honest cost is that this **takes a risk §8.5 had avoided**: that table's own comment says a kind must come from an explicit marker because inferring one is a guess, and a wrong guess routes work to a Role with a different output contract. That reasoning still holds, so the risk is taken *visibly* rather than denied — the prompt binds the choice to Thomas's **words** (the `important`/`independent_validation` discipline) with `null` → analysis for anything unclear, and the queue receipt **names the kind it read** and arrives *before* the pipeline runs, which turns a misread into a `/cancel` instead of a confident wrong-shaped answer. An unroutable kind is **refused, never defaulted**: the front desk calls `capabilities_for_request_kind` rather than keeping a second, more lenient copy of that rule — reachable only on schema/router drift, and then the refusal is the router's. The seam got its own test on purpose (front desk → entry → drain → `run_task(request_kind=…)`): both halves were already covered, and a field renamed between them would leave both green while every conversational request silently became an analysis again — the #201 failure mode exactly.
- Also done: **The compose passthrough prohibition is enforced, not documented** (`tests/test_deployment_env_passthrough.py`; 2026-07-27). `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` has always said live-trading credentials belong in the terminal of the human placing an order, "never the compose `.env`" — and that rule lived only in prose. One line added to a service's `environment:` block would have armed a permanently-restarting container (`restart: unless-stopped`) with a key that can trade, and nothing would have objected. Now eight variables (the switch, both confirmation phrases, the order key/secret, the account feed and its key/secret) must reach **neither** service, checked **per service** because per-service drift is what #245 was. Two things make it more than a list. **The bypass is covered**: every other check in that file reads the `environment:` block, so a service declaring `env_file: .env` would forward the whole file while each per-variable assertion still passed — a prohibition has to cover the mechanism, not the enumeration. **The account variables are not a safer subset**: `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` derives the order credentials from the account ones (`MVP_LIVE_ORDER_API_KEY="$BINANCE_ACCOUNT_API_KEY"`), so it is one key, and `account.py` being read-only by construction is a property of *this code*, not of a key that carries futures-trading permission at the venue. Splitting it into a genuinely read-only venue key is the only thing that changes the answer, and it would be a deliberate edit to the list. Stated in the test rather than assumed: this is **defence in depth** — no autonomous entry point can reach the order path today, so a container holding these could not trade right now; the point is that the day cycle routing lands, the credentials must not already be there. Both guards were mutation-tested (leak one variable → fail; add `env_file` → fail).
- Also done: **The live-trading env moved into the scheduler, and the guard moved with it** (`docker-compose.yml`, `tests/test_deployment_env_passthrough.py`, `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`; **Thomas decision 2026-07-27**). Hours after the prohibition above was pinned, Thomas reversed it: the eight live-trading and account variables are now forwarded from the compose `.env` to the **scheduler**, so the readiness board and the account read answer on the service without a per-session export — in particular `daily_loss_breaker`, which needs the venue's realized figure because the local outcome ledger cannot supply one. **The record is deliberately two entries rather than one rewritten one**: the earlier reasoning did not become wrong, it became a cost that was accepted, and a future reader reversing this again should be able to see what was traded. That cost, kept in the compose comment and the test: credentials in a service outlive the session that set them and return on every restart (`restart: unless-stopped`), readable by anything that can inspect the container; and it is **one Binance key** — the order credentials are derived from the account ones — so `account.py` being read-only *by construction* is a property of this code, not of a key carrying futures-trading permission at the venue. **One half did not reverse**: the operator service still receives none of it, because its loop reads no market or account data and a key that can trade would widen a blast radius for nothing — the same reasoning that keeps the signed prediction credential scheduler-only. The test therefore inverted in one direction and held in the other, driven by the same list, and both were mutation-tested (drop one from the scheduler -> the forwarding check fails; leak one into the operator -> the prohibition fails). The `env_file:` bulk-forward guard is untouched and now matters more, since it is what would hand the operator the whole file behind the per-variable checks. Unchanged by any of it, and stated in three places so it is not inferred: no autonomous entry point may import the order path, the per-machine `live_trading` grant is still required, and env alone opens nothing — which is why this is a cost rather than a capability. The grant file is now the switch that matters, since the env no longer expires with a shell. **All three of those clauses expired the next day and the entry is left standing rather than edited**, per the same "two entries, not one rewritten one" rule it argues for above: cycle routing shipped (one module may reach the order path, not none), the `live_trading` grant was removed (see the 2026-07-28 entry at the top), and env alone therefore opens everything — forwarding is now a **capability**, not a cost.
- Also done: **Progress delivery — one live status line per run** (`pipeline.STEP_*` + `PROGRESS_STAGES` + `_progress` + `run_task(on_progress=…)`, `operator.ProgressNotice` + `PROGRESS_LABELS`, `TelegramChannel.edit`, `send` returning a message id; 2026-07-28). Between the queue receipt and the deliverable there was **nothing** — on a validated run that is two model calls of silence, during which a working runtime and a wedged one are indistinguishable from the channel. The drain now sends one short line when a run actually **starts** (a different fact from "queued", which is why it is its own message) and **edits that message in place** as each stage begins. **One message however long the run**: six stages as six notifications would be worse than the silence, and editing is deliberately wired to this line only — an analysis Thomas has already read must not change under him, the same reason the ledger is append-only. **Stages that ran, not stages that were planned**: `_progress` fires from inside each branch, so a run that skipped the independent review never claims one, and a degraded search does not announce a search-and-then-nothing. **One vocabulary, not a second one for the UI**: the stage names are the pipeline's own `STEP_*` constants — the same strings the programization observer records for repetition detection — so a rename moves both consumers or neither, and a test pins `PROGRESS_LABELS` equal to `PROGRESS_STAGES`. **No percentage anywhere**, enforced rather than remembered (a test rejects `%` in any label): a model call has no denominator, and a bar that reaches 80% and sits there is a lie told slowly. **Nothing durable and nothing audited** — a `CONTROL_CHANNEL_RESPONSE` (ALLOW) on the already-verified channel, the ack precedent; notably **no `task_registry_entry` version was added** for a `progress_message_id`, because a message id that does not survive a restart does not belong in a durable record, and the alternative (editing the receipt) would have required exactly that. **Every failure direction is "the run wins"**: `_progress` swallows *every* exception rather than the typed ones (the callback reaches a network channel, and the analysis is the point of the run), a line that cannot be sent or comes back with no id degrades to the pre-existing silent behaviour rather than to a message per stage, and the **first** failed edit switches the line off for that run rather than retrying five more times into a channel that just refused. The line is closed *before* the deliverable is sent, so a finished answer never arrives under a status still reading 분석 중. Testing note worth carrying: the end-to-end seam tests need an activated Core (every stage is downstream of binding) and therefore skip on CI, so the wiring is *also* guarded by a **structural** test that never skips — it reads `run_task`'s source and asserts an emit site exists for exactly the declared stages, both directions. Mutation-checked: deleting one emit site turns it red. **One process note, because it cost a red CI run:** the local suite reported green while CI failed, because a developer checkout with no Core activation silently skips ~200 tests — including every one that exercises the drain, which is exactly what this change touches. `tests/_helpers.py` says so in as many words; it was read as full coverage anyway. The reproduction that is *not* written down anywhere and should be: clone the branch into a throwaway tree and run `scripts/ci_activate_core_for_tests.py` there — never in a tree whose history matters, because that script commits.
- Also done: **Domain console — asking the crypto and prediction stacks what they are doing** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/domain_console.py`, `/crypto status|readiness|paper` + `/pred report`, wired in `operator.handle_operator_message` with both verbs added to `CHANNEL_VERB_AUTHORITY`; 2026-07-28). Both stacks ran on a schedule and spoke only when *they* had something to say — the digest, an alert — and there was no way to **ask**. Their state was reachable only by opening a terminal on the host, so "어제 크립토 어떻게 됐어?" had two possible answers: a model narrating a store it cannot read, or nothing. **Every subcommand is a thin adapter over the module that already owns that answer** — `crypto.dashboard`, `crypto.live_readiness`, `crypto.feedback`, `predmarket.report` — and the renderer's text is passed through **untouched**, no reflowing or summarising. Writing a second rendering is the thing this module exists to avoid: two accounts of the same money will eventually disagree, and the one on the phone is the one read in a hurry. **No socket**, twice over: the dashboard's `--account` flag is the one network path in the underlying modules and it is never passed (a test greps for it), and on the real deployment the operator service is not configured with a crypto feed at all — the env is scheduler-only — so these verbs read what the scheduler *wrote*. **The console cannot trade, and that is enforced by the rule's existing owner rather than a second weaker check**: `domain_console.py` was added to `test_no_autonomous_entry_point_reaches_the_live_order_path`'s file list, and a test in the console's own suite pins that membership so dropping it cannot be silent (a passing tripwire over four files looks exactly like one over five). Two things that scan found immediately and are worth knowing: the tripwire is a plain **substring** scan, so it tripped on this module's own docstring *mentioning* an order module — crudeness is the point, since it cannot be satisfied by an import written a cleverer way — and the entry-point list existed in **two** copies (the tripwire's and the readiness board's import-graph check), which is now one named constant, because a file added to one and not the other would have left the board asserting a build state the tripwire disagreed with. **Deliberately not built:** `/crypto strategies|candidates|canaries` and `/pred groups|observations|opportunities|schedule` from the original sketch. Each would need **new rendering surface** rather than an adapter, and new prose about live money on a chat surface deserves its own change with Thomas reading the diff — the four that shipped are the ones that already had a renderer.
- Also done: **Clarification resume — `frontdesk_turn.v0.4` carries the answer back into the request** (`clarification_texts` on the SUBMIT_TASK payload, `_clarification_texts`/`_compose_request` + the per-segment verbatim check and the composed receipt, role definition 0.4.0 → 0.5.0 and its registry hash; 2026-07-28). `CLARIFY` asked a question and **ended the turn**. When Thomas answered, his answer was just a new message — and the verbatim rule (a submission must be a substring of **one** message) made the combination he actually meant the only thing that could not be submitted. That was measured before anything was built: with "Prediction 데이터 분석해줘" → *(CLARIFY)* → "7일", submitting the original alone passes and loses the period, submitting the answer alone passes and is meaningless, and submitting **both** is rejected. **The verbatim rule was not weakened — it now runs per segment, and one failure refuses the whole submission**: checking the composed string would pass a paraphrase glued between two real quotes, and submitting only the segments that passed would silently drop the very answer this exists to carry. Nothing of the front desk's own is added (no labels, no `clarification:` prefix), so what changed is *which* of his words are submitted, never *whose*. The receipt **quotes the assembly** when more than one segment survives — composing is the one thing he cannot see from his own scrollback, since he said two things and one request went in — and it arrives before the pipeline runs, so a wrong assembly is a `/cancel` rather than a wrong answer. Segments are deduplicated **across the whole request**, and that shape was found by a test rather than by reasoning: the first implementation deduplicated `clarification_texts` and `request_text` separately, so a model that helpfully echoed the request into the list produced it twice; a repeat is never dangerous (every segment is still his words) but it reaches the specialist as an emphasis he did not write. **The estimate for this item was wrong and the correction is the useful part:** it was written up as needing `task_registry_entry.v0.3` plus a `WAITING_FOR_INPUT` state and a resume path. It needs **no registry change at all** — the clarification happens *before* anything is queued, so there is no entry to suspend, and a state nothing can produce is worse than no state. Mid-run clarification (a pipeline that pauses to ask) is a different, much larger feature and remains unbuilt.
- Also done: **1d candidates were FRAGILE by sample size, not by edge** (`crypto/market_data.MIN_FACTORY_BARS` + `factory_candle_target`; 2026-07-29). Replay depth became a **calendar** span to fix the fast timeframes — a flat 500-bar window is five hours at 15m, so all three walk-forward slices land in one session and every candidate mined there is single-regime by construction. That fix left the slow end starved in the mirror image: 500 days is 48k bars at 15m and **500 at 1d**, while the robustness scorer counts **trades**, which scale with bars and not with calendar span. Every 1d lineage therefore scored on ~350 replay bars and ~16 trades, under the scorer's critical trades-per-parameter, so a 1d candidate was FRAGILE **whatever its edge** — the verdict was reporting the window, not the strategy. Measured on the 25 legacy 1d strategies still trading, same specs and same cost model, only the window changed: at 500 bars **0 ROBUST / 20 FRAGILE**, holdout CONFIRMED 11; at the floor **12 ROBUST / 1 FRAGILE**, holdout CONFIRMED 15. The floor is **one-sided** — it may only lengthen a window, never shorten one — so every timeframe the calendar reasoning already bound stays bound and only 1d moves (4h is nearest at 3,000 bars and does not). 2,000 is **what the venue can answer**, not a round number: the shortest routed USD-M perpetual history is ~2.1k daily bars (SOLUSDT), and a higher floor would collect short and score a window it never received. Consequence worth knowing before reading any 1d verdict: the candidate store now holds 1d evidence scored at **two depths**, and unlike cost basis (`pool.cost_basis_rank`) there is no tier marking which — a depth ranking analogous to the cost one is the follow-up this leaves behind.
- Also done: **The pool had no way to retire one strategy** (`runtime/mvp_runtime/crypto/retirement.py`, `scripts/retire_strategies.py`, `lifecycle.operator_retirement_decision`, `permission.build_strategy_retirement_permission_decision`; 2026-07-29). Found while deduplicating the 1d pool: five entries are the **same rule**. The miner appends conditions that are always true (`low < high`; `mark_index_basis_bps <= 0.0`, a constant 0.0 in this runtime's feature builder), and the rule hash covers the condition **sequence**, so a no-op mints a "new" strategy that clears the duplicate guard — and the padded clone outranks its own base rule, so `route_entries` picks the tautology. Retiring one needed a door that did not exist. `update_statuses` is the narrow pool mutation — status only, never membership — but it accepts only lifecycle decisions off **realized performance**, and a redundant strategy produces none: routing picks ONE strategy per context, so the slot-holder is the only one that ever generates the outcomes the automatic path would need in order to demote the others. The promotion door is the opposite extreme: replace mode rebuilds every entry with a hardcoded `PAPER_ACTIVE`, so dropping one strategy **reactivates every terminally SUSPENDED member**. That is not a reading of the code — it was simulated against a copy of the real pool: 16 reactivated, 57 lifecycle counters reset, and 53 of 73 rows needing the stale-cost-basis escape, which would have made the escape the normal door. **Zero new authority**: same `RUNTIME_GOVERNANCE` scope and P4 level as a promotion, because "this one only stops trading" is not a reason to ask Thomas less; what differs is the effect's width, and the action identity says so, so a retirement approval can never execute as a promotion (tested). The content hash binds **slot AND lineage AND rules** — `update_statuses` keys on the display `strategy_id` while the thing being retired is a lineage, so binding the id alone would retire whatever holds that name at execute time. Verified end to end against a copy of the real 78-entry pool with a real approval: membership 78 → 78, exactly the named entries changed, **zero non-status drift** on the other 73, all 16 previously-SUSPENDED still SUSPENDED. That last one is pinned as a regression test, because it is the whole reason the verb exists.
- Also done: **The board never said work was waiting on a human** (`pool.promotable_backlog` + `PROMOTION_BACKLOG_ALERT_THRESHOLD`, `operator.last_inbound_at` + `INBOUND_SILENCE_ALERT_DAYS`, two warnings and two fields in `crypto.dashboard.build_status`; 2026-07-29). Promotion went untouched from 07-26 to 07-29 while sixteen `crypto_factory` schedules kept minting every morning, and the daily report went out on each of those mornings reading `warnings=0`. **Two independent causes, and only fixing both helps.** The first is that nothing counted the queue: the report described the pool and the paper record, never the decision sitting undone. The second is worse and was found while chasing the first — the control channel's **inbound** half had received nothing since 07-26T10:23:42Z while outbound worked perfectly, so the report itself was delivered every day, three `/approve` messages reached nobody, and three approvals expired unanswered. The operator **heartbeat passed throughout**, correctly: the loop really was turning. `getMe` and `getWebhookInfo` were clean, `pending_update_count` 0, and a `peek()` (the cursor-preserving read that already existed for the halt path) confirmed Telegram had nothing queued — the messages had never arrived. Only the poll cursor knows this, and it knows it as a **side effect**: `_save_offset` runs exactly when an update is fetched, so its write time IS the last inbound. That is now **recorded** in the file rather than inferred from mtime, with mtime kept as a named-as-weaker fallback for cursors written before the stamp — a rebuild or a chown moves mtime without a message arriving, and this number decides whether an approval can still reach the runtime. **What is counted is not what exists.** The factory mints dozens of candidates a day, so a threshold on the candidate count fires every morning and is ignored by the second week; the backlog applies the same chain `--list` and the promotion gate apply — promotable cost basis, recomputed ROBUST, holdout CONFIRMED, positive expectancy **at current rates**, and one row per (family, symbol, timeframe). That last key must also cover the **active pool**, which the first implementation got wrong and the real store caught within minutes: excluding pool members by `strategy_rule_hash` while collapsing candidates by lineage counts one slot twice, so promoting one re-mint leaves its siblings to resurface as fresh backlog forever — 7 reported where 3 were waiting. One key now does both, and the case is a regression test. **Nothing here refuses anything**: the proposer's backlog cap makes a fire skip, this only speaks, and the promotion door stays exactly as manual as it was. The never-received warning is gated on an operator registration existing, so a fresh checkout and every mock-channel deployment stay silent instead of carrying a permanent line that means "configured as intended".
- Also done: **The duplicate guard was an identity check on the record, not on the strategy** (`pool.canonical_rule_form` / `behavioural_fingerprint` / `semantic_duplicate_groups` / `assert_no_semantic_duplicates` / `near_duplicate_groups` / `pool_candidate_records`, wired into `promotion.request_promotion`, `scripts/promote_strategy_candidates.py` and `scripts/import_crypto_history.py`; 2026-07-29). Every dedupe in the crypto stack keyed on `strategy_rule_hash`, which covers the condition **sequence** — so two ways of holding one strategy under a different hash both walked straight through, and both were sitting in this store. **Reorder the conditions**: `evaluate_spec` folds them with `all()`/`any()` over the full result list, no short-circuit, so order cannot change what a spec does; one such pair (S2646/S2881) was already in the candidates. **Append a condition that never discriminates**: the source system's rule miner minted four BNBUSDT lineages that are one base rule plus `low < high` or `mark_index_basis_bps <= 0.0` (a constant `0.0` in this runtime's feature builder), and because the padded clones scored *higher* than the base rule, `route_entries` — which picks ONE strategy per context — was routing a tautology while the real rule never traded. The cost of a seated clone is not a doubled position (the router prevents that) but a **permanently inert pool member**: it holds a slot, never produces an outcome, and so the lifecycle can never collect the evidence it would need to demote it. **Two exact tests, no heuristics.** Rule form is order-insensitive and needs no evidence, which is the only thing available at the import door, where source rows arrive with no backtest at all. Behaviour keys on `evidence_input_sha256` first — comparing outcome aggregates across different windows compares two different questions — and then on every aggregate the store keeps; agreeing on all of them over ~100 trades is not a coincidence worth designing around. **What is deliberately NOT refused** is as much of the design as what is. A partial aggregate set fingerprints as `None` (agreeing on the two numbers a sparse row happens to record is not the same claim, and treating it as one made every sparse row everyone else's duplicate — caught by two existing promotion tests). A zero-trade spec fingerprints as `None` (they all "agree"). Members sharing a rule hash are skipped, because one lineage re-scored on a new window is one strategy measured twice — the append-only store exists so both rows can live, and without this the guard flagged all 25 re-scored 1d lineages against their own originals. And a **near** duplicate — same window, same trade counts, R differing in the last decimals, which is what the SOLUSDT pair S2965/S3094 actually is — is *reported in the operator list view and never gated*, because "almost certainly one strategy wearing two rules" is a judgement and this module refuses only on what it can prove. Measured against the live 570-row store: 2 groups refused (exactly the reordering pair and the padded group), 5 near groups reported, zero false positives. The escape is `--allow-duplicates` on both doors, recorded on the ledger like every other one.
- Also done: **The budget said which symbols and nothing read it** (`live_order.normalize_symbols` + the `allowed_symbols` gate in `evaluate_live_order_guard`, threaded through `live_entry.plan_live_entry`, `live_route` and `scripts/place_canary_order.py`; 2026-07-29). `live_trading_budget.v0.1` has carried `symbol_allowlist` since the first record: `build_live_trading_budget_record` normalizes it and **refuses an empty one**, so the field was validated, self-hashed and stored — and then dropped. `resolve_live_order_limits` copies only the numeric caps into `LiveOrderLimits`, and no other module read the list. **The caps bound how MUCH could be traded while nothing bound WHAT.** Measured on this machine the day it was found: the registered budget named `["BTCUSDT"]` while the active pool held strategies on ETHUSDT, DOGEUSDT, BNBUSDT and SOLUSDT, `MVP_LIVE_TRADING=real`, and `live_readiness` reported READY with autonomous routing WIRED. The two never disagreed out loud because only one was ever consulted, and the tests agreed with both: `test_..._live_budget.py` covered construction — case normalization, empty-list refusal — and no test asked whether a non-allowlisted symbol could reach an order. **The default is empty, which blocks everything**, the same fail-closed shape as `budget_registered=False` and chosen over a required kwarg for the same reason it was there: a caller that does not state the scope must be *refused*, not silently unbounded. That default made six existing tests fail on the first run, which is the gate proving it binds — each was a helper composing a guard call the way a caller does, and each now passes the scope the way a caller does. **The canary door is not exempt**, unlike the promotion gate it sits next to: a canary is a smaller real order, not a different kind of one, and its evidence is counted **globally** (`clean_canary_order_count` has no symbol filter), so a canary aimed outside the budget would leak that scope into the autonomous gate as evidence for a symbol it never tested. The allowlist is **re-normalized on read** rather than trusted from the record, because a hand edit is the one way an un-normalized list arrives and a case difference must neither widen the scope nor narrow it; a bare string is refused rather than admitted character by character. What this deliberately does **not** do: no cap arithmetic changes (a test pins every cap field equal across an allowed and a refused symbol), and widening stays a **re-registration** — a new self-hashed record with a new id — rather than anything derived from the active pool, because deriving scope from the pool would turn every paper promotion into a live-money decision with no operator action and no record.
- Also done: **The oi_\* families judge the hour on a value that steps once a day** (`crypto/oi_store.py`, `market_data.OI_INTERVAL_*` + an `interval` parameter on `open_interest_history`, wired into `cycle.attach_feeds` and surfaced on the board; 2026-07-29). Four of the ten lineages promoted that morning are `oi_squeeze_long` / `oi_unwind_short` — the strongest thing the factory currently mints, the best of them +0.99R at current rates — and every one reads a **daily** OI series. `_open_interest_event_columns` says the consequence outright: *"a change here is day against day regardless of the bar size the caller will align onto."* So a 1h or 4h entry times an hour off a value that moves once. **Not a vocabulary problem**, which is where the investigation started and was wrong: the raw level is deliberately unmintable (evidence-only), and the mintable pair — `open_interest_change_pct` and `open_interest_zscore`, the latter a rolling z-score **of the level** — is already the per-symbol normalization a shared parameter space needs. **Measured on the vendor rather than assumed**, and the measurement is the whole decision: hourly history begins ~84 days back, 15min ~21, 5min ~7, daily 500+; windows at 140–200, 340–400 and 460–520 days back return **empty**, so it is a retention wall and not the ~2000-row response cap that first looked like one (Binance's own `openInterestHist` is shallower still — 500 rows = 21 days at `1h`, HTTP 400 on any older window). **So the switch could not simply be made.** Every timeframe below 1d replays `FACTORY_DEPTH_DAYS` = 500, and backtest and live must read the same feature source; pointing the feed at `1hour` today leaves 83% of each replay window indeterminate, the family closes too few trades to earn a verdict, and the change deletes the evidence behind the strategies it was meant to improve. The only way past a vendor's retention window is to stop depending on it, so this ships the store and **nothing else**: `snapshot` is untouched, a test pins that the hourly series never reaches the features, and the daily series stays the one source both halves read. Three shape decisions worth carrying: the vendor request is throttled to **once per symbol per hour** inside the store (the pool fan-out is twenty contexts at a 15-minute cadence — without it, eighty requests an hour to record five readings); `coverage` is a **span**, not a row count over 24, because a gap in the middle would flatter a row count into claiming depth the factory could not replay, and `gap_hours` rides alongside so the span is never read as completeness on its own; and reads never raise on damage, unlike `pool.read_candidates`, because this store backs a number on a board rather than an approval an operator signs — under-reporting coverage is the safe direction. Eligibility is 500 days of the **weakest** symbol, printed as a field rather than a warning (it will read "축적 중" for roughly a year, and a warning true every morning for a year is how a board teaches its reader to skip the warning block), and the flip itself stays an explicit change. One process note: the accumulation starts on the first cycle **after this deploys**, not when it merges — the clock and the deploy are the same event, which is the entire argument for building the store before the feature change is wanted.
- Also done: **Forty vendor requests to read ten symbols, and the last two paid for it** (`market_data.PerSymbolFeedCache`, constructed in `cycle.run_pool_cycle`; 2026-07-30). Both Coinalyze series are per-**symbol** — `liquidation_history` and `open_interest_history` take a symbol and a day count and nothing else — but `attach_feeds` runs once per (symbol, **timeframe**), so the shipped 20-context fan-out asked for each symbol's series four times. Downstream the redundancy was invisible: the series is aligned onto each timeframe's candles *after* the fetch, so four identical responses produced four correct frames, and 400 consecutive cycles reported zero degraded feeds. **It stopped being invisible the moment the hourly OI store added five more requests per fire.** First fire after that deployed, read in context order: BNB/BTC/DOGE `ok`, ETH/SOL `degraded` on **every** Coinalyze series — not just the new hourly one, but the daily open interest and the liquidations the paper and live routers already depend on. Contexts are visited in a fixed order, so a per-window request cap always lands on whoever is last: the early symbols look healthy and the late ones silently lose features. That is the failure mode worth carrying, more than the count — a partial rate limit reads as "three symbols are fine" rather than as an outage. The fix is a **cache rather than a slower loop or a bigger cap**, because within one fire the answer to "what was this symbol's daily series" cannot legitimately differ between the 15m context and the 4h one; caching is behaviour-preserving where throttling would trade freshness for headroom. Two properties are load-bearing and tested: the cache is constructed **per fan-out** and discarded, so it can never serve a stale day to a later fire; and **failures are cached too**, since a vendor that just refused this symbol will refuse it again in the same second and re-asking three more times per fire is how the cap was reached — the refusal is re-raised unchanged so every context still records its own reason code. A wiring test drives `run_pool_cycle` itself, because a cache nobody constructs caches nothing and the symptom of deleting that one line does not appear until a vendor starts refusing the tail. Net: 40 requests become 10.
- Also done: **The five breaker limits were constants nobody had authorized** (`guards.RiskLimits` + the relaxation bounds, new closed schema `crypto_risk_limits.v0.1` + `runtime/mvp_runtime/crypto/risk_limits.py` + `scripts/register_crypto_risk_limits.py`, wired in `cycle.py` and one board row; 2026-07-30). `RISK_PER_TRADE`, `DAILY_MAX_LOSS_R`, `WEEKLY_MAX_LOSS_R`, `MAX_CONSECUTIVE_LOSSES` and `MAX_DRAWDOWN_PCT` were hardcoded — the right starting posture, ported from the source system, and the *same* gap `live_budget` closed for the LP3 order caps: not adjustable without a code change and a deploy, and not a **record**. Nothing hashed, nothing auditable, no statement of who authorized which number or for how long, and a limit could change between two cycles with the only trace being a diff. **Why a new schema and not a field on `live_trading_budget.v0.1`** (the reuse-first question, asked first and answered against reuse): that record is venue-scoped, symbol-allowlisted and live-only, and these limits bind the **paper** path too — `cycle.py` runs the same C4 verdict for both, so a machine that will never place a live order still judges every entry on them. Putting them in the live budget would mean registering a live-trading budget to change paper behaviour, and the two records' expiry semantics point opposite ways: an expired budget correctly means *no live trading*, while an expired limit reverting to a default would **loosen** a breaker. That second half is the trap that shaped the whole resolution rule. The obvious `except ToolError: use the defaults` is fail-**open** in the one direction that matters: an operator who *tightened* a breaker would have it silently widened back by the very failure meant to be conservative. So a record that cannot prove itself — unparseable, hash-mismatched, out of bounds, or past its window — **refuses**; `cycle.py` turns each into no-new-position with the reason code, and only a *missing* file resolves to the defaults, because that honestly means nothing was registered. **Relaxations expire, defaults do not**: the validity window is what makes a widened breaker a re-authorized decision instead of a permanent edit nobody revisits, and a machine that registers nothing keeps the defaults forever with no window to lapse. The bounds (`risk_per_trade ≤ 0.02`, `daily ≥ -6R`, `weekly ≥ -15R`, `consecutive ≤ 10`, `drawdown ≥ -25%`) live in `guards.py` beside the numbers they bound, are **refused never clamped** — the posture `live_budget` takes on its 200 USDT ceiling, for the same reason: a clamp trades under limits nobody chose — and are **one-sided**, so tightening is unbounded and needs no approval path at all. They are re-checked in `run_risk_guard` itself, not only at parse: a caller that constructs `RiskLimits` directly and skips the record layer gets a fail-closed verdict rather than an unbounded one, which is the same lesson as the `plan_live_entry(verdict=None)` fail-open (#200) — enforce where the value is *used*. A daily limit looser than the weekly one is refused as incoherent rather than accepted as lax, because the weekly breaker would trip first in every history and the daily number would bound nothing while still reading like a limit. **The verdict now says which limits judged it** (`source`, `limits_id`, `record_sha256` and all five numbers), and `cycle.py` keeps that in the ledger even though it drops the rest of the verdict for size: "ALLOW" no longer states what was allowed, so a row nobody can re-check against the numbers in force at the time is not an audit trail. What this deliberately does **not** do: no default changed (a test pins all five to the source values), it grants nothing and enables no trading, and it does not touch the live money path's own caps — `live_trading_budget.v0.1` still solely owns per-order notional, daily count, exposure and the USDT loss limit. The one board row it adds is green when nothing is registered, unlike every other row, because the defaults are the supported steady state and a fresh machine must not read as unready over a record it is not expected to have; it goes red only for a record that exists and cannot be used, which is exactly the state where the C4 guard silently refuses every entry.
- Also done: **The hourly OI throttle measured the wrong clock and never skipped** (`oi_store.record_refresh_attempt` / `read_refresh_marks`, `is_due` re-keyed onto the attempt; 2026-07-30). The store shipped claiming one vendor request per symbol per hour, and its own status line said otherwise on the first fire: four `appended` results per symbol with **nothing appended**. `is_due` measured from the newest stored **reading**, and the vendor's newest complete hour is always at least an hour behind the clock because the forming hour is dropped — so "the newest reading is over an hour old" was true for almost every minute of every hour, and all four contexts of a symbol re-asked inside one fire. The claim in the previous entry was wrong in the way worth naming: what actually bounded the requests was `PerSymbolFeedCache`, a **different mechanism with a different lifetime** (one fan-out), so the two never substituted for each other — the cache stops one fire asking twice, and only this stops the next fire asking again fifteen minutes later. Fixed by recording the **attempt** rather than inferring it from the result, in a small per-symbol JSON map (the `routing_marks` idiom). Two directions are deliberate and tested. The attempt is stamped **before** the request, so a fetch that hangs or raises still counts — a marker written only after a successful return would leave the failing path unthrottled, and the failing path is the one that most needs it: a refusing vendor asked four times a fire is sixteen an hour per symbol, which is how the request cap was reached in the first place. And a corrupt marks file reads as **no marks**, i.e. "ask again" — fail-open toward collecting, the opposite of the direction this store's row reads take, because a marks file that read as "recently attempted" would silently stop the accumulation while the board went on reporting whatever coverage it already had. Cost of a persistent failure is now one ask an hour; the delay to a self-heal is bounded by the same hour, which the vendor's ~84-day retention absorbs.
- Also done: **A SUSPENDED entry could not say why it stopped trading** (`lifecycle_reasons` / `lifecycle_retired_by` on the pool entry, `demotions_by_reason` on the board; 2026-07-30). Reading the live pool after a promotion, five 1d entries showed `SUSPENDED` with `lifecycle_consecutive_failures: 0` and nothing else, and that pair was read — wrongly, here — as a performance demotion. Zero cannot be one: a metric suspension needs a streak of at least `suspend_consecutive`, so zero is impossible on that path. What produces zero is `operator_retirement_decision`, which carries the count **forward untouched** — a strategy that never failed is retired at zero. The five were duplicate rules an operator removed on purpose the day before (`reasons: ["operator_retired"]`, with a retirement_reason naming them as one lineage padded with always-true conditions), and the only place that said so was `control_events.jsonl`. So the two demotions a reader most needs to tell apart — *the metrics condemned this edge* and *a human removed a duplicate* — presented identically on the surface everyone opens first. `update_statuses` now writes the decision's `reasons` and, when there is one, its `retired_by`, alongside the timestamp and decision id it already recorded. Three details are deliberate. They are written **only on a status change**, so an entry always carries the reasons for the status it is actually in rather than for the last evaluation that touched it. An absent field and an empty list stay **different answers** — absent means an older runtime wrote the row, empty means the transition named no reason — and the board renders the first as `unrecorded` rather than inventing a reason for it, which is the state the five live entries remain in, since adding fields to durable state outside a door is not something this runtime does retroactively. And the mutation stays as narrow as it was trusted to be: a test pins spec, rule hash, score and generation unchanged across a transition, because the reason `update_statuses` is allowed near the pool at all is that it cannot smuggle a promotion.
- Also done: **The backlog counted candidates the runtime could never judge** (`pool.days_to_lifecycle_window` + `MAX_DAYS_TO_LIFECYCLE_WINDOW`, a `deferred_unjudgeable` list on the backlog and a line on the board; 2026-07-30). Every filter the backlog applied asked whether the **backtest** was believable — cost basis, evidence depth, recomputed ROBUST, holdout CONFIRMED, positive expectancy at current rates. None asked whether a **forward verdict was reachable**, and that gap emptied the pool the same day it was found: an operator retired 63 of 89 strategies (`approval_c1b8eeb7681c08579648`), including all eleven promoted in the preceding twenty-four hours, on exactly that argument — *"at current rates a 1d lineage needs 122d and a 1h lineage 29d to reach the 20-trade WARNING window, so neither can ever be auto-demoted; today no own lineage exceeds 13 trades and zero strategies are eligible for any lifecycle rule."* **The mechanism is worth stating because it inverts the intuition:** `route_entries` picks ONE strategy per context, so promoting another lineage into a context does not add trades — it **splits the same trades across more lineages**. Adding strategies therefore dilutes the outcome stream the lifecycle needs, which is how a pool of 89 reached a state where nothing in it could be graded. A note in this session's earlier reasoning was wrong in exactly that way: it judged the cost of a promotion as slot contention (small, the paper book holds 20) when the real cost was attribution rate. The measure is read from the candidate's **own** evidence — `closed_count` over `bars_replayed` is trades-per-bar, and the timeframe converts it to trades per day — so no cross-store join is needed and the number a reader sees is derived from the row they are judging. Measured across the store: median days to twenty trades is 15m **7**, 1h **25**, 4h **110**, 1d **371**, which reproduces the operator's own figures closely enough to trust the basis (they measured 1h at 29). The fourteen-day threshold is a **recorded judgement, not a derivation** — it is the line that reproduces that retirement's 15m-only shape, and the constant says so and says to change it by deciding again. Two directions kept honest: an unknowable rate (no bars, no closed trades, an unknown timeframe) is **deferred rather than assumed fast**, since a lineage that closed nothing over its replay window is the clearest case of one that will never be evaluated; and the deferred ids are **named, never silently dropped**, because a count that hid them would read as "nothing is waiting" when what is true is "nothing the runtime could grade is waiting".
- Also done: **The index price series was asked for by symbol, and the venue wants the pair** (`market_data.DERIVATIVE_KLINE_SYMBOL_PARAMS`; 2026-07-30). `derivative_price_klines` built one parameter set for all three endpoints and named the instrument `symbol=`. Two of the three accept that; `indexPriceKlines` does not, because the index is quoted per **pair** — one index for BTCUSDT however many contracts settle against it — and the venue enforces the distinction. Measured live: `markPriceKlines symbol=` 200, `premiumIndexKlines symbol=` 200, `indexPriceKlines symbol=` **400 `-1102 Mandatory parameter 'pair' was not sent`**, `indexPriceKlines pair=` 200. **The partial success is why it survived.** Every cycle since the series landed reported `index_prices: degraded` beside `mark_prices: ok` and `premium_index: ok`, which reads as a feed blip rather than as a malformed request — the same shape as the rate-limit incident earlier the same day, where the first three symbols returning `ok` made a systematic failure look intermittent. Worth carrying as a rule: **a feed that fails for one member of a set and succeeds for its siblings is a request problem until proven otherwise**, because a real outage does not pick favourites. The cost was not an outage either, which is the second reason nobody chased it: `index_price` and `mark_index_basis_bps` fell to indeterminate, so specs naming them simply never matched. That is fail-closed and safe, and it is also exactly wrong for what C13 was for — the fabricated constant-0.0 basis it exists to remove was replaced by a permanently **absent** one rather than by a real measurement, so half the increment silently did nothing. The key now sits in a table beside the path table, so a fourth kind has to answer both questions instead of inheriting an assumption; a test pins the two tables' key sets equal, another pins that a request names the instrument exactly once, and another walks more than one page — because the parameter set is rebuilt per page and a fix applied only to the first would leave every backfill page malformed while looking fixed.
- Also done: **Auto-demotion was unreachable, so the promotion door now caps the pool** (`pool.MAX_ROUTABLE_STRATEGIES` / `MAX_ROUTABLE_PER_CONTEXT` + `routable_context_map` + `assert_pool_within_size_cap`, wired into `scripts/promote_strategy_candidates.py` as its fourth guard with an `--allow-oversized-pool` escape; 2026-07-30). Found by asking why a **-13.25R** strategy family was still routing. It was not a threshold problem: `run_lifecycle` returned `PAPER_ACTIVE` with **empty reasons** for all 67 routable strategies, because every demotion rule reads through `_full_window`, which returns `None` until a rolling window is FULL — 20 trades for WARNING, 30 for PROBATION, 50 for SUSPENDED, 100 for ARCHIVE. Attribution is by lineage, and 86 own outcomes had scattered across **29 lineages, the largest holding 13**. Not one strategy in the pool was eligible for any rule. **The safety net had never once been in a position to fire**, and the only breaker that did fire was the weekly loss limit, which is what tripped and started this. The cap is on the **routable** set, not on membership — a SUSPENDED entry neither routes nor splits the evidence, and the day this landed the pool file held 89 rows with 5 routable, so a membership cap would have been both wrong and unenforceable. `MAX_ROUTABLE_PER_CONTEXT = 1` is the part that does the real work, and it is the *router's own* behaviour that justifies it rather than a preference about diversity: `route_entries` returns `ranked[0]`, so a second entry in a context **cannot trade** — it wins the slot only by `champion_score`, which on this machine was **anti-correlated** with realized R (the 0.83–0.92 scorers had never traded; the five 15m strategies that earned +12.84R scored 0.41–0.43), it halves the rate at which either lineage reaches a judgeable window, and if the two disagree on direction the context fails closed on `BLOCK_DIRECTION_CONFLICT` and *neither* trades. The existing duplicate guard had already written down half of this — "the router picks ONE strategy per context, so a duplicate takes the slot and then never trades" — and the generalisation is that nothing about that argument was specific to duplicates. `MAX_ROUTABLE_STRATEGIES = 20` is one per context on today's 5×4 grid and is **not** implied by the per-context cap: it is what still binds when the symbol set grows, so widening coverage becomes a deliberate edit rather than a silent return to a pool nothing can judge. Checked on the **merged** result, not the batch — in add mode the incumbents are what make a two-candidate promotion oversized, so judging the batch alone would pass every promotion that ever mattered — which has the consequence that an approval granted while the pool had room can refuse later if the pool grew in between; that is the check working, because the approval authorizes candidates and never a pool shape measured at ask time. Refused, never truncated (the `live_budget` ceiling posture): dropping the overflow silently would install a pool nobody chose, with the victim decided by list order. What this deliberately does **not** fix, stated in the code so the next reader does not mistake the guard for a complete answer: a 1d lineage produces 0.16 outcomes/day per context, so even at an exclusive slot it needs **~122 days** to fill a 20-trade window (1h ~29, 4h ~33, 15m ~10) — un-demotable by arithmetic, a property of the timeframe that no pool-size cap can reach. Two existing tests changed rather than were patched: one factory run mines every candidate for the same context, so "promote two of them" is precisely what the per-context cap refuses, and both now say so with the escape and a comment rather than quietly encoding the old shape.
- Also done: **The breaker was calibrated against a number with the frictions removed** (`paper.build_outcome_record` applies `cost.apply_cost_model`; `live_pnl.R_BASIS_INTENT_NET` + `R_BASES_NET_OF_COSTS`; `gross_result_R` / `fee_cost_r` / `slippage_cost_r` / `maker_fee_cost_r` on every new paper outcome; 2026-07-30). Found while asking whether `champion_score` was broken, and the answer was that **it was not** — the comparison that indicted it was. `champion_score` is a ROBUSTNESS score (sample adequacy, temporal consistency, regime breadth, parsimony, cost robustness — not one term is expected return), and it rated the 15m cohort lowest largely through `cost_robustness = 0.000`: over 184 candidates their median backtest expectancy is **-0.264R NET**, i.e. nothing survives fees. The pool's realized R said the opposite, and the two were never the same statistic: the factory backtest has always charged costs, while `paper.py` did not import `cost` at all. **Every paper stop-out was recorded at exactly `-1.0000R`** — the tell, since paying to be stopped out costs more than the risk planned. Measured cost per trade from the backtest's own `cost_summary`: **15m 0.285R, 1h 0.133R, 4h 0.061R, 1d 0.030R**. Applied to the book: the week the weekly breaker tripped read **-5.53R** over 43 trades whose costs came to ~7.8R, so the loss it reacted to was about **-13.4R** against a -5R limit — the breaker under-measured by ~2.4x and will always be late by the fee drag, most on the fastest timeframe, which is where the trades are. The whole own book is **+3.53R gross / about -14.0R net**. This was a deliberate boundary, not an oversight: `cost.py` ported the source's rule that costs belong to backtest scoring and paper measures *"pure signal quality on intended fills"*, and that was right while paper R was only a measurement. It stopped being right when this port wired paper R into the C4 loss breakers and the C10 demotion ladder — money-safety decisions read a statistic missing a term worth more than the limit they enforce. `REMAINING_WORK`'s PM2 already planned the move; this is its fee half, not the fill model. Shape: `settle_trade_plan` stays the single authority on GROSS (its exactly--1R stop convention included) and the record subtracts only what the cost model says the legs cost — `apply_cost_model` could re-derive gross and would agree today, which is exactly why it is not asked to. `win_loss` is judged on NET, because a trade that clears its stop but not its costs is not a win and counting it as one is how a losing book reports a healthy win rate. **No backfill, and the label says so**: an old row keeps `entry_price`/`exit_price`/`direction` but not `risk`, and the model is denominated in risk-per-unit, so the 86 legacy rows can only be labelled `intent` beside the new `intent_net_of_costs` — a window spanning today mixes bases and under-states its losses, which is stated on the constant rather than left for a reader to discover. What this does NOT change: `factory.backtest_spec` is untouched, live R still carries venue slippage without fees (a third basis, deliberately not folded in), and the retirement executed hours earlier survives the correction — the five kept 15m lineages are **+17.67R gross over 30 trades, about +9.12R net**, while the five retired alongside them were negative before costs.
- Also done: **The breaker's state was reported every day and its CHANGES never** (`crypto/breaker_watch.py` + `scheduler.KIND_BREAKER_WATCH`; 2026-07-30). `crypto_report` already renders the C4 verdict daily, which answers *"is it blocked right now"* for whoever reads that morning's message and never answers *"when did it release"* — the question an operator actually waits on, because a level reported every day buries the one day it flipped among the days it did not, and the flip is what decides whether the runtime is trading at all. So this is an **edge** trigger, kept as its own kind rather than a field on the report for exactly that reason: same inputs, different question. It runs the real `guards.run_risk_guard` against the real `risk_limits` and the same own-paper-plus-bridged-live composition `cycle.run_crypto_cycle` uses — a watch that assembled its inputs differently would eventually announce a state the runtime is not in, which is worse than no watch. Three decisions worth keeping: (1) **it speaks only on a change**, keyed on the VERDICT — allow/refuse and which breakers are named — never on the R numbers, because a watch that fired on every settlement is a trade feed and the operator already has one; (2) **the first fire always announces**, since with no marker there is no previous to differ from and silence on a fresh deploy is indistinguishable from a watch that is not running; (3) **an undelivered announcement is not marked as said** — the scheduler runs it with `persist=False` and writes the marker only after `notify_operator` returns, so a revoked telegram grant costs a retry rather than a transition nobody was told about. The marker read is deliberately fail-OPEN on a corrupt file, against this repo's usual posture: nothing here authorizes anything, so there is no permission to fail closed on, and the worst case is one redundant announcement against the alternative of silencing the thing whose whole job is to speak. It also reports the `r_basis` mix, and names the caveat only when the window actually has one: paper R became net of costs earlier the same day, so a window spanning that boundary under-states its losses by the legacy rows' unpaid costs, and "-4.8R, clear" means something different depending on how much of that window predates the change. Verified against the live state: the current book renders BLOCK_NEW_POSITION on `weekly_loss_limit_breached` with `intent 69, unlabelled 17` and the mixed-basis note, and the same state evaluated at 2026-08-03T00:30Z — the Monday the rolling week resets — renders `CRYPTO BREAKER RELEASED`.
- Also done: **Every naked entry was handled correctly, and that is why the loop ran unbounded** (`live_order.LiveBracketFailureBreaker` + `MAX_CONSECUTIVE_BRACKET_FAILURES`, the `LIVE_ENTRY_BRACKET_BREAKER_TRIPPED` door in `plan_live_entry`, `live_route._record_bracket_outcome`, `scripts/clear_bracket_breaker.py`; 2026-08-03). The runtime's first two autonomous live entries filled and their protective stops were both refused; `live_leg` Rule 2 closed each position again, safely, for 0.12 USDT in fees. Nothing was broken about the response — and nothing counted it, so the next signal was judged exactly like the first. The failure mode worth naming is that **each iteration ends tidily**: there is no incident, no halt, no unprotected position, just a small fee and a runtime that cannot hold a trade, repeating as fast as the daily order budget refills at UTC midnight. Three decisions shape the fix. It counts **consecutive** failures reset by a bracket that actually rests, so one venue hiccup does not latch the door while a broken path shuts it at two. It **does not expire** — a breaker on the same clock as the budget it bounds would bound nothing, which is precisely how the original loop resumed each day. And an entry that never reached the bracket (`ENTRY_REFUSED`, `ENTRY_NOT_CONFIRMED`) leaves the streak alone rather than clearing it, because those outcomes never tested the protective path; a "not a failure means success" reading here would have disarmed the breaker on the most ordinary cycle there is. The reset is an operator script demanding a written reason, not a timer, since the failure it follows is by definition one nobody has explained yet — and the venue's own `error_detail` (#426) is copied onto the breaker record, which outlives the container whose logs made the first two uninvestigable. This does not fix the rejection; it makes the next attempt the last one that costs anything if the answer is still "broken".
- Also done: **A trading-switch grant cleared whatever stop it found, and the policy named a gate that no longer exists** (`switch_bridge.stop_ref` / `stop_summary` + the `STOP_CHANGED` / `STOP_NOT_NAMED` refusals; `permission.build_trading_switch_permission_decision(stop_ref=, stop_summary=)`; `governance/GOVERNANCE_POLICY.yaml` `financial:` comment; 2026-08-05). Two records that described a control other than the one in force. **The grant.** `control.CMD_RESUME` takes no argument saying which stop to lift — there is one control state and it clears whatever is in it — so an approval recording only the verb described its effect only until something else touched the switch. A grant minted against the assistant's own kill stayed spendable against an operator's later, unrelated one, which is an approval for one intent releasing a stop placed for another, in the direction of starting something nobody approved. Mode could not have caught it: kill, resume, kill again reads KILLED at both ends. The stop's identity is now in the ask's fingerprint and re-derived from live state under the lock that applies the resume. The cost is stated where it is paid: `/stop <task_id>` rewrites `updated_at` without changing `mode`, so an unrelated stop request between ask and spend also invalidates the grant and costs a re-ask — the cheap side of a door whose whole asymmetry is that starting is the expensive verb. This binds a grant to *a* stop; it does **not** make the effect domain-scoped, and the ask's risk text now says the resume releases every scheduled kind that stop was holding. **The policy.** The `financial:` block named "the per-machine `live_trading` safety-flag grant, which is Thomas-minted, TTL-capped and revocable" as what authorizes a machine to place a live order. That grant was removed on 2026-07-28 (`safety_gate.env_only_authorization`; it is not in `_KNOWN_FLAGS`) and `MVP_LIVE_TRADING=real` has been the whole gate since — the commit that removed it bumped `policy_version` to 1.2.0 and left this comment describing the old world. The harm is specific rather than cosmetic: a reader failing closed off this block looks for a grant file to delete in order to revoke, finds none, and concludes live trading was never armed. The block now names what is load-bearing and points at `crypto.live_readiness` as the authority for what is live on a machine, which a committed file cannot be. Editing it re-hashed the two read-only replay bundles that bind the policy by content (`refs`/`sha256`/`governance_binding` plus each bundle's own `integrity.bundle_sha256`) — the kernel preflight caught it as `BUNDLE_FINGERPRINT_MISMATCH`, which is the binding working.
- Also done: **A maintenance burst held the pass the money path needed** (`scheduler.RISK_KINDS` + `MAINTENANCE_PASS_BUDGET_SECONDS`, risk-first ordering and a non-claiming deferral in `run_due`, `deferred` on the summary and the tick line; 2026-08-05). `run_due` is one sequential pass in one process, and the next tick cannot begin until it ends — so the kinds sharing a *due time* were never the whole story: a long pass delays whatever falls due **during** it, which lands in a later tick and is invisible to any same-tick reading. That is why this went unseen for so long, and the first pass at measuring it here reproduced the mistake: grouping fires by tick showed `crypto_pipeline` had **never** shared a pass with another kind, which reads as 'no problem' and is not. Measuring the thing that matters instead — the gap between consecutive pipeline fires against its own 900s cadence — found it late by **116s, 240s and 349s** on three mornings, each one inside the ~08:09Z window where fifteen `crypto_factory` schedules share a due time and held a single pass for 600-664s (74% of a pipeline period). **Measure the victim's latency, not the queue's composition.** The fix is ordering plus a budget, and the budget is the load-bearing half: ordering only helps a risk kind already due when the pass starts, which is not the case that bit. A pass stops *starting* non-risk fires once it has spent 60s; a fire already running is never interrupted, because cutting a factory mid-write trades latency for a torn record and this loop's at-most-once contract rests on a claimed occurrence completing or being recorded failed. So the guarantee is bounded, not absolute: at most ONE maintenance fire can now stand between a due risk kind and its execution, where before it was every fire that shared the pass. A deferred occurrence is deliberately **not** claimed — unlike the kill-switch branch it sits below, which claims and *drops* — so it stays due and the next pass runs it: the cadence slips one tick and nothing is lost. It is counted as `deferred` on the summary and appended to the tick line rather than folded into it, because `docker-image.yml` greps the literal `fired 0, skipped 0, failed 0` and a budget that quietly shortened that line would read as 'nothing was due'. `RISK_KINDS` is asserted by a test rather than described: `candle_archive` is the longest fire in the system and the one the budget most needs to be able to defer, so it must never drift into the set.
- Also done: **The budget's own record said that it bound, not what it cost** (`pass_elapsed_ms` + `pass_budget_ms` on the `ACTION_DEFERRED` event; 2026-08-07). #596 put the deferral on the ledger, which closed the evidence gap the budget had — before it, "how often does it bind" was answerable only from `docker logs`, capped at 10m×3 and discarded by a container recreation several times a day. What a bare count still cannot say is whether **60 is the wrong number**, and that is the only decision the record exists to serve: a pass that bound at 61s and one that bound at 600s write the identical row, and they argue opposite things — the first that the budget sits on the boundary and a larger value would stop binding, the second that one fire is longer than any budget worth setting, so the number is not the lever at all. Carrying the spend separates them from one row. `pass_budget_ms` is recorded rather than read back off the constant, for the reason `mutation_event` carries `previously_enabled`: a value inferred from today's source silently re-scales every row written before the source changed, and a record that invites the reader to infer is the failure this whole event stream exists to end. Volume is bounded by the work that caused it rather than by the 30s tick — the branch is reachable only after the pass has already spent the budget, so a pass that fires nothing can never defer.
- Next: every roadmap phase (R0.5–R10) and both deferred R4 items are done. Consumption is scoped to memory promotion + candidate-role trials; widening it to any other scope, or to Executor handoff / external / financial effect, remains a separate explicit Thomas decision (and `CONSUMPTION_PREVIEWED` still needs the deferred `execution_request.v0.1`). Candidate-role **promotion** (registry `status: active` after a convincing trial record) is its own explicit Thomas decision.
